## Wargame-concordance Row 10 — VERIFY findings: WP assembly-phase radio silence

**Manuscript claim contexts confirmed as read:**
- `drafts/04-norms.md` lines 186–190 (the G-2 Hvidt/Holt exchange): "Assembly silence is his doctrine, not his lapse; we have taught it to each other for twenty years... silence kept by forty thousand men has a shape — it has edges, where the silent nets end and the talkative ones begin, and edges can be plotted."
- `drafts/16-the-bank.md` lines 211–218: intercept annotation — "bridging and assault-crossing equipment moving forward by night opposite the narrows; radio silence broken twice, both times by equipment serials, both times silenced mid-transmission."
- Shelf: `reference/soviet-operational-art.md` holds only WWII maskirovka *radio-deception* (fake radio nets spoofing OOB, Suzhda 1943, lines 305–314) — deception, not emission control. `reference/oob-verification.md`: no radio/emission/silence hits. Concordance's self-assessment ("NOT a direct emission-control extraction") was accurate.

### Graded verdicts

**(a) Radio silence / emission control on assembling &amp; pre-contact formations as standing WP practice — CONFIRMED-primary.**
FM 100-2-1, *The Soviet Army: Operations and Tactics* (HQDA, 16 Jul 1984), Ch. 3 "Command and Control," p. 3-4:
- "Radio is the principal means of communications, **especially when in contact with the enemy.**"
- "**Wire is used extensively in the defense and in the preparatory phase of offensive actions.**"
- "Operator discipline is strict, operating procedure is of a high order, and security precautions are to be observed minutely."

The doctrinal ground truth: WP formations not yet in contact — i.e., assembling and second-echelon forces during offensive preparation — run on wire and messenger, with radio held down; radio "comes alive" at contact. That is emission control / de facto radio silence during assembly, and it is documented, standing practice, not a lapse. (Source: https://irp.fas.org/doddir/army/fm100-2-1.pdf , archive text: https://archive.org/stream/fm100-2-1/fm100-2-1_djvu.txt .) Caveat: the manual expresses this as wire-preference + "radio when in contact," not the verbatim phrase "radio silence during assembly" — the manuscript's framing is a faithful corollary, not a quoted clause.

**(b) Silence enforced until attack-preparation fires / H-hour — CONFIRMED-secondary.**
This is the direct corollary of (a): radio is principal "especially when in contact," and wire carries the "preparatory phase," so nets open as the attack (artillery preparation / assault crossing) begins. Reinforced by the surprise/security principles in FM 100-2-1 p. 2-2/2-4 ("Maintain complete security of combat operations"; "Deceive the enemy. Attack from an unexpected direction at an unexpected time"). The specific "until preparation fires begin / H-hour" trigger is a well-founded inference from doctrine, not a verbatim quote I could secure (I could not extract Ch. 5 "The Offensive" verbatim — see limitations). Grade held at CONFIRMED-secondary rather than primary for that reason.

**(c) Serial-number breaches silenced mid-transmission as texture — PLAUSIBLE (well-founded texture).**
Not a documentable doctrinal fact, and not claimed as one — it is scene texture. It is consistent with FM 100-2-1 p. 3-4 ("operator discipline is strict... security precautions observed minutely") and with the OPSEC-indicator literature, which flags exactly this failure mode: JP 3-54 App. C lists "alert and maintenance personnel using handheld radios or testing aircraft or vehicle radios" as an observable indicator (https://irp.fas.org/doddir/dod/jp3-54/3-54c_ac.htm). A brief net-control-suppressed emission from a moving-forward equipment serial is realistic; the "silenced mid-transmission" detail reads as disciplined net control, not error. No overstatement.

**(d) "Silence has edges that can be plotted" as period SIGINT tradecraft — CONFIRMED-secondary.**
Traffic analysis + direction-finding + electronic-order-of-battle inference is WWI/WWII-vintage and fully mature by 1983 — not anachronistic. Wikipedia/standard-tradecraft synthesis (traceable to NSA Callimahos, *Introduction to Traffic Analysis*, https://www.nsa.gov/portals/75/documents/news-features/declassified-documents/cryptologic-spectrum/intro_traffic_analysis.pdf):
- "Traffic analysis can be performed even when the messages are encrypted."
- "using direction finding (DF) tools, the position of the emitter is locatable, the change of locations from one point to another can be deduced."
- "Who talks to whom – can indicate which stations are 'in charge' or the 'control station.'"
- "A lack of communication – can indicate a lack of activity, or completion of a finalized plan."
And OPSEC doctrine treats imposed silence itself as an observable indicator: JP 3-54 App. C — "instituting radio silence when close to the time of starting an operation... periods of silence may catch adversaries' attention." The manuscript's "edges, where the silent nets end and the talkative ones begin, and edges can be plotted" is precisely inference-by-contrast (silent zone vs. active nets) plus a DF baseline — and ch. 4 explicitly names the "direction-finding baseline," which is textbook. Faithful rendering of real tradecraft.

### Net assessment

**Row 10 closes CONSISTENT.** The doctrinal ground truth supports the manuscript on all four sub-claims: WP pre-contact/preparatory-phase emission discipline is documented primary-source doctrine (FM 100-2-1 p. 3-4 — radio principal *when in contact*, wire in the *preparatory phase*), so the G-2's "his doctrine, not his lapse... we have taught it to each other for twenty years" is accurate, and the NATO mirror (traffic analysis, DF, radio-silence-as-indicator) is equally well documented. **No manuscript sentence overstates the doctrine.** Two honest hedges for the record: (1) the primary manual frames assembly emission control as *wire-preference + radio-when-in-contact* rather than a verbatim "radio silence during assembly" clause — the novel's phrasing is a sound corollary, not a quotation; (2) the exact H-hour trigger (b) and the serial-breach detail (c) are inference and texture respectively, both well-founded and neither overstated.

**Could not verify:** I could not extract FM 100-2-1 Ch. 5 "The Offensive" verbatim (the archive.org full-text summarizer truncated before p. 5-1), so the night-movement / assembly-concealment / artillery-preparation-opens-the-attack passages are inferred from the C2 chapter and the surprise/security principles rather than quoted. I did not pull Voroshilov Lectures, Reznichenko's *Taktika*, or Savkin directly; the FM 100-2-1 primary passage plus the OPSEC/traffic-analysis doctrine was sufficient to grade every sub-claim without them. If a stronger primary anchor for (b) is wanted later, the target is FM 100-2-1 Ch. 5 and FM 100-2-2 on march/movement security.

Sources: [FM 100-2-1 (FAS)](https://irp.fas.org/doddir/army/fm100-2-1.pdf) · [FM 100-2-1 full text (archive.org)](https://archive.org/stream/fm100-2-1/fm100-2-1_djvu.txt) · [JP 3-54 App. C OPSEC Indicators](https://irp.fas.org/doddir/dod/jp3-54/3-54c_ac.htm) · [NSA Callimahos, Intro to Traffic Analysis](https://www.nsa.gov/portals/75/documents/news-features/declassified-documents/cryptologic-spectrum/intro_traffic_analysis.pdf) · [Traffic analysis (Wikipedia synthesis)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_analysis)</result>

## Net answer

**A specific military Belt-ferry capacity figure (battalions/day, lane-m, vehicles/sailing) still does NOT exist in reachable open sources — confirmed negative.** But the hunt closed the more important gap the residual was really circling: **the requisition/prioritization mechanism and its legal-planning instrument are now reachable, named, and documented**, and the scholarly work-of-record on "DSB in Danish total defense" is identified. One quantified ferry-lift equivalence surfaced, but it is Warsaw-Pact-side and rough, not a Danish Belt number.

The single decisive source is a **SECONDARY, freely readable** scholarly article:

&gt; Ulrik Vestergaard Jensen &amp; Peer Henrik Hansen, **"Kold krig på skinner — DSB og dansk infrastruktur i en østtysk optik,"** *Jernbanehistorie* (Danmarks Jernbanemuseum), 2019. Full PDF: https://tidsskrift.dk/jernbanehistorie/article/download/119240/167048/247380

It has a dedicated section, "Færgehistorien – myte eller realitet?", and closes on precisely the Belt-ferry question.

---

## What is now reachable (with grades)

### 1. The planning/legal instrument — the mechanism the residual wanted (SECONDARY→PRIMARY, partially reachable)

The ferries were pre-designated for mobilization prioritization by a real, named, classified document chain:

- **DSB "Beredskabsinstruks," in force from August 1976**, classified *Til Tjenestebrug* ("For Official Use," lowest level), kept hidden at stations. It **replaced** the earlier standing orders: *"instruks for danske stats- og privatbanestationers forhold ved det militære forsvars forøgelse af fredsstyrken,"* **December 1962**, and its update of **1 September 1970**.
- On its content (article, ll. 474–480):
  &gt; *"Instruksen omhandler i hovedsagen, hvordan DSB skal forholde sig ved forøgelse af fredsstyrken, dvs. deltage i mobilisering. Det er beskrevet, hvordan materiel, færger, tog, perroner og andet skal prioriteres med forrang for indkaldt personel til forsvarets enheder."*
  &gt; **Translation:** "The instruction essentially governs how DSB is to act upon augmentation of the peacetime force — i.e. take part in mobilization. It describes how **materiel, ferries, trains, platforms and other assets are to be prioritized, with precedence for called-up personnel to the armed forces' units.**" (Point 2.6 *Ødelæggelser*/"Destruction" covers denial so materiel does not fall intact to the enemy.)

This is the requisition/prioritization mechanic in the 1980s totalforsvar framework: **ferries are explicitly named among the assets the railway prioritizes for mobilization movement.** The wider legal umbrella is the *kongelige forholdsordre* of 6 March 1952 plus Civilforsvarsloven (1949); wartime *fuldmagtslov* provisions on "ekspropriation og udskrivning af privat ejendom, køretøjer og materiel" (expropriation and requisition of private property, vehicles and materiel) were the intended statutory basis (koldkrig-online.dk, "Beredskabssystemet").

The **Beredskabsinstruks (1976)** and its 1962/1970 predecessors are cited to primary copies (article note 45 quotes its p. 1‑1 verbatim). This is the document DK should chase for a figure.

### 2. The scholarly work-of-record (SECONDARY — trace it)

&gt; **Asbjørn Rune Riis-Knudsen, *Totalforsvar på skinner — DSB's rolle i totalforsvaret af Danmark under den kolde krig*, speciale (MA thesis), SDU Odense, 2013.**

The article says DSB's total-defense role "er grundigt behandlet" (is thoroughly treated) here and leans on it repeatedly (its pp. 20 for the Beredskabsinstruks lineage, 23, 48, 60–63 for the ferry/Storebælt-bridge defense debate). A published article version exists and appears reachable: https://tidsskrift.dk/jernbanehistorie/article/view/26887 ("Totalforsvar på skinner"). **This is the most likely single source of an actual Danish capacity/timeline figure if one was ever committed to open print.**

### 3. The one quantified ferry-lift number found — but WP-side, not Danish, not the Belt (SECONDARY)

&gt; *"Færgernes betydning kom blandt andet til udtryk i øvelsen 'Val-74', hvor fire danske færger skulle flytte en kampvognsdivision til Holland... [de danske færger indgik] som en betydelig sømilitær transportressource."*
&gt; **Translation:** "The ferries' importance was expressed among other things in exercise **'Val-74', where four Danish ferries were to move an armored division to Holland**... the Danish ferries figured as 'a significant naval-military transport resource.'" (Sourced to **DIIS, *Danmark under den kolde krig*, vol. 2, p. 604**; ferry harbors as espionage/strike targets, vol. 2 p. 619.)

Caveats: this is a **Warsaw Pact** exercise assumption (the Kystfront using captured Danish ferries onward to the Central European front), a division→Holland lift, **not** a Danish plan and **not** the Halsskov/Korsør run. As a rough order-of-magnitude it says *~4 ferries ≈ 1 armored division* in period military reckoning — usable only as coarse triangulation, and explicitly an enemy planning figure.

### 4. Theater rail comparanda (SECONDARY, context only)

Kjeld Hillingsø's figures for the *opposing* side, useful as a scale check:
&gt; *"...i DDR forberedt mellem 400 og 448 tog og syv landeveje til tung militær transport... Da en division krævede 60 tog, betød det omtrentligt... at fem-seks divisioner skulle fremføres med jernbane."*
&gt; **Translation:** In the GDR, **400–448 trains and seven highways** were prepared for heavy military transport near the inner-German border; **a division required ~60 trains**, i.e. ~5–6 divisions moved forward by rail. (Hillingsø, in Hedegaard (red.) 2008, p. 88.)

### 5. Protection / escort — partial (SECONDARY/TERTIARY)

No dedicated Belt air-defense or mine-escort throughput plan surfaced. What exists: ferries and ferry harbors were registered *nøglepunkter* under FE's **Nøglepunktssektion** (key-point section, which deliberately blurred them on public maps); rail assets were guarded by **Jernbanehjemmeværnet** and station **bedriftsværn**; harbors were standing East-bloc espionage targets. The "ferries-as-minelayers dual-role" legend was **investigated and could not be documented** (authors consulted Gen.Lt. Kjeld Hillingsø, naval historian Søren Nørby, Tom Wismann): *"det ikke... har været muligt at finde dokumentation for, at de danske DSB-færger havde en dobbeltrolle"* — no evidence found, though not disproven.

---

## Direction note (relevant to chs. 2, 15–16)

The reachable sources foreground the *opposite* Belt flows from the manuscript's premise: **Jutland's tanks (Jyske Division) rail SOUTH to Schleswig-Holstein**, and NATO reinforcements land at Esbjerg/Hanstholm/Frederikshavn and move EAST to Zealand (~3.5–5 h by rail once the bridge existed). The manuscript's **Zealand-brigades → Jutland/LANDJUT** flow is not contradicted — it is separately corroborated by the CIA-1989 finding already on the shelf (Zealand-raised Jutland Brigade earmarked to reinforce LANDJUT) — but note that no reachable source narrates the Zealand→Jutland lift by ferry with any capacity figure. The **prioritization mechanism (Beredskabsinstruks) is direction-agnostic**, so it underwrites the manuscript's requisition premise regardless.

---

## Pursuit list for DK (print/archive tier — where a Danish Belt figure would actually live)

1. **Riis-Knudsen, *Totalforsvar på skinner* (SDU speciale, 2013)** — full text via SDU thesis repository/library; article version at https://tidsskrift.dk/jernbanehistorie/article/view/26887. Highest-probability holder of a Danish capacity/timeline figure.
2. **DSB "Beredskabsinstruks" 1976** (+ predecessors, Dec 1962 and 1 Sept 1970 station instructions) — likely **Rigsarkivet, DSB fonds**, and/or **Danmarks Jernbanemuseum** archive (the museum holds related BV/hærtransport material; the article's authors worked from museum holdings). This is the document that would carry any planned prioritization/throughput.
3. **DIIS, *Danmark under den kolde krig* (2005)** — free PDFs: bd. 2 https://pure.diis.dk/ws/files/27985/KKBind2.pdf (pp. 604, 619 — Val-74, ferry harbors), bd. 3 https://pure.diis.dk/ws/files/27986/KKBind3.pdf (pp. 556–558, 564–565); bd. 1 pp. 479, 483, 520. Worth a direct page-pull for any ferry-lift arithmetic (I did not fetch the volumes themselves this round).
4. **RA, FE's arkiv**, "V. Diverse sager (afklassificerede) (1940–1990)," **kasse 7–11** (East German *Verwaltung Aufklärung* terrain-reconnaissance material; FE-notat 24 June 1995) — for the ferry/harbor targeting and enemy capacity assumptions.
5. **Militært Tidsskrift / krigsvidenskab.dk** digitized archive and **Rigsarkivet, Forsvaret fonds** — staff studies of **Landsdelskommando ØST/VEST (ELK/VLK)** on Zealand↔Jutland reinforcement are the natural home of an actual Danish Belt lift capacity/timeline; none surfaced digitized, so this is a physical-archive request.

**Bottom line:** the requisition *mechanism* and its instrument are now sourced and quotable (Beredskabsinstruks 1976; the ferries named for mobilization priority) and the scholarly treatment is identified (Riis-Knudsen 2013), which is a real upgrade over the prior "NOT FOUND" state — but the discrete **military capacity figure** remains print/archive-tier and unfound, with items 1 and 2 above the sharpest leads.</result>

## RESULT: The RUSSWO gate is closed — primary USAFETAC data found for two theater stations, one of them a Schleswig-Holstein airfield with a table explicitly built from the Schleswig RUSSWO.

### The document
**Air Weather Service Climatic Briefs: Europe**, USAFETAC, prepared July 1981. DTIC accession **AD-A118450**.
- DTIC (`apps.dtic.mil/sti/tr/pdf/ADA118450.pdf`) returns **403 to automated fetch** — but the full scan is freely readable on the Internet Archive: **https://archive.org/details/DTIC_ADA118450**
- The briefs use the standard AWS "CAV FREQ (%)" (Ceiling-And-Visibility frequency) panels: for each month and 3-hour LST block, the **percent of hourly observations with ceiling OR visibility below** a stated floor. **Flyable fraction = 100 − printed value.**
- Two in-theater stations carry the full ceiling/visibility ladder: **Karup, Denmark** (leaves 0016–0017) and **Hohn, Schleswig-Holstein** (leaves 0047–0048). Jever and other northern stations are in the same volume if wanted.

### Grade: PRIMARY, period-correct
- **Karup** (WMO 06060 / EKKA, N56 18 E009 07, elev 170 ft): sources = Karup "N-Summary" Jan 1951–Nov 1971 + USAFETAC DATSAV 1966–76.
- **Hohn** (WMO 10038 / EDNO, N54 19 E009 32, elev 39 ft — in the LANDJUT rear, near Rendsburg): sources = **"Schleswig RUSSWO, POR Jan 1964–Dec 1973"** + DATSAV 1966–76. This is literally a Schleswig/Jagel RUSSWO-derived table. Period of record is 1950s–1970s — squarely pre-1983, **no climate-drift caveat** (better than the 1961–90 normals for this purpose).

### November numbers — % of hours BELOW minimum → **flyable %**
(ALL-HOURS row, November column, read off the scanned tables)

| Ceiling/visibility floor | Karup: below → flyable | Hohn: below → flyable |
|---|---|---|
| &lt; 3000 ft and/or &lt; 3 NM | 52 → **48%** | ~52–55 → **~46%** |
| &lt; 1500 ft and/or &lt; 3 NM | 41 → **59%** | 45 → **55%** |
| &lt; 1000 ft and/or &lt; 2 NM | 32 → **68%** | 35 → **65%** |
| &lt; 800 ft and/or &lt; 2 NM | 29 → **71%** | 27 → **73%** |
| &lt; 500 ft and/or &lt; 1.5 NM | 22 → **78%** | 22 → **78%** |
| &lt; 300 ft and/or &lt; 1 NM | 13 → **87%** | 12 → **88%** |
| &lt; 100 ft and/or &lt; 0.2 NM | 4 → **96%** | 2 → **98%** |

The two stations agree within a few points at every rung, and agree with the DMI/DWD cloud-cover normals already in `november-climate.md` (69–72% mean cloud, 13–14 overcast days). Diurnal signal is **weak** (midday 12–17 LST only ~3–8 points better than night) — consistent with frontal/advective stratus rather than radiation fog, so day-vs-night flyability differs only modestly, unlike summer.

### Recommended bracket: "flyable-day fraction, November, Baltic Approaches"
The answer hinges on where the tactical minimum is set, so I recommend the instrument carry **two categories matching the doc's own thresholds**:

- **Tactical-air / visual-CAS minimum ≈ ceiling ≥ 1000 ft (300 m) / vis ≥ 3 sm** (the question's "300 m/3 mi" ≈ "1000 ft/3 mi"): **flyable ≈ 62–65% of hours.** The table's nearest rung (1000 ft/2 NM) gives 65–68%; the wargame's 3 sm (≈2.6 NM) is a slightly stricter vis floor, dropping it ~2–4 points. **Use 63% ± 5.**
- **Conservative "good visual attack" minimum ≈ ceiling ≥ 3000 ft / vis ≥ 3 NM** (roll-in from altitude, clean acquisition): **flyable ≈ 46–48%.** **Use 47% ± 4.** This is the "well under half the days" the reference file inferred — that inference was right *for the high minimum* but too pessimistic for a low-level A-10-style minimum.

**Confidence: HIGH.** Two independent, period-correct USAFETAC tables (one RUSSWO-sourced for Schleswig-Holstein) converge, and match the held cloud-cover normals.

### Caveats to record with the number
1. **Hours, not calendar days.** These are % of hourly obs — ideal for a sub-daily sortie model. "Days with at least one flyable window" would be *higher*; "fully-flyable days" *lower*.
2. **"and/or" semantics:** printed value = ceiling-below **OR** vis-below; flyable = 100 − printed.
3. The doc's vis floors (2/3 NM) differ slightly from 3 statute miles (2.6 NM); interpolate as noted.

### Implication for the reference file
Open item 1 in `reference/november-climate.md` (and the §1/§5 "assumption pending the RUSSWO-type table" flags) can be **discharged to a sourced number**. Karup and Hohn are both named-acceptable stations; Hohn additionally satisfies the "Schleswig-Holstein airfield" preference and is RUSSWO-derived.

### If DK wants the deeper document (optional pursuit)
The AWS brief gives only the **marginal** "and/or" thresholds. For the full **joint ceiling-versus-visibility cross-tabulation**, the underlying **Schleswig RUSSWO, POR Jan 1964–Dec 1973** is the parent source (behind the Hohn table). Pursue as a standalone RUSSWO on DTIC/NTIS; no free copy surfaced today, but its existence and period are now confirmed by AD-A118450's source line.

**Verification image URLs** (single-page extraction from the JP2 zip):
- Karup summary — `https://archive.org/download/DTIC_ADA118450/DTIC_ADA118450_jp2.zip/DTIC_ADA118450_jp2%2FDTIC_ADA118450_0016.jp2`
- Hohn summary — `.../DTIC_ADA118450_0047.jp2`; Hohn addendum — `.../DTIC_ADA118450_0048.jp2`

(Working images saved in scratchpad as `p0016.png`/`p0017.png` [Karup] and `h0047.png`/`h0048.png` [Hohn]; nothing written into the repo.)</result>

## ASSISTANT

All four verify-queue items are worked, integrated, and committed — eight commits this arc. The queue that stood at the top of the third-draft agenda's research section is now: two closed, one discharged with primary data, one worked to its open-source floor with your pursuit list sharpened. Summary:

**1. Danevirke (line-edit 12.5) — clause wrong, reword needs your ruling.** Every documented assault on the wall — Henry the Fowler 934, Otto II 974, Wrangel 1848, the Prussians/Austrians 1864 — came from the south, the direction it faces; no invader ever came from the north, and the only flanking threats came from the east over the Schlei (the same flank our fictional geometry worries at Sehestedt, a happy convergence). The drafted irony is dead, but the true history offers a better one: *the wall always faced the right way and was stormed or outflanked every time anyway — it never got to win its south-facing battle until this war.* Research is banked in `reference/danevirke.md`. Options for the clause (original: "dug against invasions that had come, every one of them, from the other direction, and standing its watch now facing south, which the guidebooks had always said was the way it faced and the war had finally confirmed"):

- **(A) My recommendation:** "…dug against invasions that had come, every one of them, out of the south, and never once been held by it, standing its watch now facing south, which the guidebooks had always said was the way it faced and the war was finally asking it to prove." — Historically exact (stormed 934/974/1848; abandoned 1864), no added dash, and "asking it to prove" is true that night and paid off when the narrows hold.
- **(B) Minimal:** "…every one of them, from just this quarter…" with the close changed to "…and the war had finally let it hold."
- **(C) Fuller:** "…every one of them, out of the south it watched, and had overrun or outflanked it before it could make its stand…" with "…the war had finally let it prove." — richest, but longest and adds a dash against ch. 12's budget.

**2. WP assembly radio silence — closed CONSISTENT, primary-sourced.** FM 100-2-1 (1984) p. 3-4: wire carries "the preparatory phase of offensive actions," radio is principal "especially when in contact." The G-2's "his doctrine, not his lapse" stands exactly as written, and his "silence has edges" tradecraft is textbook (Callimahos traffic analysis; JP 3-54 lists imposed silence as an OPSEC indicator). Concordance row 10 updated; extraction on the shelf.

**3. RUSSWO gate — discharged with primary, period-correct data.** *AWS Climatic Briefs: Europe* (USAFETAC 1981, AD-A118450, free on archive.org) carries CAV FREQ panels for both Karup and Hohn — and Hohn's is literally built from the Schleswig RUSSWO, 1964–73 record, so no climate-drift caveat. November flyable fractions: **~63% ± 5 of hours at a visual-CAS minimum** (1000 ft/3 sm), **~47% ± 4 at a conservative roll-in minimum** (3000 ft/3 NM). I spot-verified the Karup panel against the actual page image before banking. The instrument's visual-fraction ASSUMPTION flag can come off — and your standing browser item for this is deleted. Notably, the old shelf inference ("well under half the days") was right at the high minimum and too pessimistic at the low-level one — which retroactively fits ch. 12's under-the-cloud rearming being the day's one working answer.

**4. Belt-ferry beredskab — figure confirmed unfound at open tier; mechanism now sourced.** The DSB *Beredskabsinstruks* (in force August 1976) explicitly named ferries among assets prioritized for mobilization movement — ch. 2's requisition minute now rests on a real, named instrument rather than plausibility. The work most likely to hold an actual capacity figure is identified (Riis-Knudsen, *Totalforsvar på skinner*, SDU 2013), and one coarse triangulation surfaced: the WP's own Val-74 exercise assumed four Danish ferries lift an armored division. Pursuit list in `transport-1983.md` §9; the ferries-as-minelayers legend is ruled undocumentable, for whatever scene-texture temptation it might ever have posed.

The one decision waiting on you is the Danevirke reword. Everything else from this arc — both registers, the ch. 13 seam fix, the silence-template ration, the cringe-rule memory, and the four research closures — is committed and reflected in `planning/status.md`.

Are they going to feel like they got a good deal for the time, attention, investment, etc. they put in? ## ASSISTANT

**On NEPS — yes, it's relevant, and it's a near-miss we got lucky on.** The short version of what I verified and banked in `transport-1983.md` §10:

The system is real and bigger than a footnote: ~650 km of military POL pipeline from Frederikshavn (main depot, with an offshore tanker import point ~7 km out to sea) down the spine of Jutland to the Kiel Canal area, built 1952–70, ~15 pump stations, carrying jet fuel, diesel, and gasoline to the Jutland and North German air bases and barracks — Karup, Skrydstrup, and Aalborg among them. The NATO literature gives the southern terminus as **Hohn** — which is, by pure convergence, our alternate-HQ woods and the climate station whose RUSSWO table we just banked. Better still for us: the **1982–84 expansion** — a branch to the Fredericia refinery and an enlarged import facility at Esbjerg — is under construction or barely finished in November 1983. Fredericia and Esbjerg are both already load-bearing addresses in the text.

Three findings for the scenario:

1. **Nothing on the page is contradicted.** Chapter 2's eleven hundred requisitioned civilian tankers read correctly as the *distribution* lift — pipeline heads to formations — which a pipeline requires rather than replaces. And the text's road-competition arithmetic (ch. 8) is ammunition-led, which NEPS doesn't carry. We were protected by having kept the fuel claims vague in exactly the right way.
2. **The gap is an absence, not an error.** The corps' ambient fuel picture is all trucks; a Bogason-grade reader might notice the pipeline never exists. One clause would cover it.
3. **The one candidate placement is chapter 6, Aakjær's inventory of intact things.** The pump stations belong on that list under either reading of the enemy — a red who wants a working peninsula spares them; a red strangling blue air hits them first — and the inventory is already that scene's engine, so the clause would earn its keep rather than brief the reader. That's flagged as a candidate for your ruling, not applied. If you hit the NEPS passage in Bogason again, the highest-value spot-check is whether he says anything about *wartime vulnerability or red targeting assessments* of the line — that would decide which way Aakjær reads it.

The wargame needs nothing: bulk POL lives inside the CAL-3 supply abstraction.

Sources: [koldkrig-online.dk NEPS-linjen](https://koldkrig-online.dk/anlaeg/neps-linjen/), [Kystmuseet Bangsbo Fort](https://www.kystmuseet.dk/besoeg-os/kystmuseet-bangsbo-fort/neps-linjen), [NATO Pipeline System topic page](https://www.nato.int/en/what-we-do/deterrence-and-defence/nato-pipeline-system), [OSW commentary](https://www.osw.waw.pl/en/publikacje/osw-commentary/2025-07-30/fuelling-defence-expanding-natos-pipeline-system-to-eastern).

**The disclosure, first, since it's the first thing the book hands you.** The front matter says the prose was drafted by a language model, directed and edited by a human, with the whole making kept in a public ledger. What that did to my trust at the start was simple: it turned me into an auditor. I began hunting for tells — uniform competence, borrowed texture, the absence of lived grain — and I want to be honest that the hunt cost me most of chapter 1, which I read with my arms crossed. By the ENDEX wash-up scene, when Brauer asks what the forty minutes at Hohn cost at Esbjerg, I had uncrossed them, because that question is a real question, specifically imagined, and no pastiche generator asks it. By the end the balance had moved a long way back — but not all the way, and the residue is precise: the book's one genuine stylistic vice (see below, the maxim monotone) is exactly the vice I'd predict from the method, and the disclosure means I can never fully rule that it's a default rather than a choice. Also noted: a book about honest ledgers, error annexes, and provenance, prefaced by a note declaring its own ledger honest, is either elegant or a little too pleased with the rhyme. I'll call it elegant, narrowly, because the book earns the theme in blood before it claims it in front matter.

**1. THE SPEND.** The heaviest tax was geography, and it ran from chapter 3 to about chapter 13. Lammers' six-places speech (ch. 3, the midnight conference) — bridge pair, ferry sets, Vamdrup, the E3 — asks you to hold a transport network in your head that the page never draws. It got worse at Friedrichstadt (ch. 7): road bridge, rail swing-bridge, Nordfeld ferry, Yellow-3 crossing the war's grain — I was sketching on a receipt. The narrows day (ch. 12) taxed me hardest of all: the western shoulder versus the Schlei shoulder is the hinge of the whole chapter, of Holt's error, and I had to reread the 0805 paragraph to be sure which side of the map I was standing on. The second tax is a sentence-type: the coined maxim. This book mints aphorisms at a rate no real headquarters ever achieved — "a claim is a hope with a format," "a transcript is a photograph of a dance," "credit is trust with a date on it" — and each is good, and the density is a spend, because by chapter 15 every scene detonates its exit-line on schedule and my attention flinched in anticipation instead of surprise. Third tax, mild: keeping the day-of-war count aligned with November's dates around chapters 10–12.

Where the spending was effortless: the cast, surprisingly — twelve-odd recurring names carried by staff function (the G-2 is the wit, the G-4 the accountant, plans is the silence), never once confused; the teleprinter capitals, which read faster than prose and pay compound interest by chapter 19a; and the enemy chapters (4, 11, 17), which cost nothing and returned everything.

**2. THE BUY.** What I own now, in my words: a working understanding of why organizations die of their own reporting — that a depot counting heads is not a division standing in Schleswig, that every green board is green *against* something, and that the something is usually the plan's opinion of itself. I own the difference between being right and being solvent, which chapter 20 states and chapters 12 and 19 prove — the corps wins while its prize instrument is wrong by fourteen hours, and the book makes you feel the fourteen hours in a specific division's casualty meter. I own Roloff: a man who inks a report line down the street where his son learned to ride a bicycle and does not let the pen pause, and whose one sentence about himself ("The report line down my street held... I would like that noted somewhere it counts," ch. 19a) is the best line in the book. And I own the bleakest good on the shelf: the mirrored ledger. Rylski keeps honest books in the beech wood for the same reasons Holt keeps them at Rendsburg, and in December (ch. 20) Rylski's honesty is collated into a prosecution exhibit while Holt's is fought into the record over Bjelke's courtesy. Same virtue, two institutions, opposite prices. I'll be telling friends about that, and about Zawadzki putting on his reading glasses to consider the folder (ch. 17) — the most quietly frightening nuclear scene I've read in years precisely because it's conducted as staff work.

**3. OVERPAYING.** Chapter 6, the middle stretch — Witzel walking the request-procedure ladder rung by rung ("the procedure is the policy... like a lobster pot") — is the one place the book briefs at me. The locked-door conceit is honest about what it is, but I paid seminar rates for material chapter 17 later delivers as drama, and I'd skim it on reread. Chapter 10, the second Rahn telephone call: the mentor delivering enumerated wisdom ("First... Second, practically...") for the third time in ten chapters; Rahn is the book's most conventional device, the oracle in Eckernförde, and each call costs a little more suspension than the last — the ch. 17 refusal-to-bless redeems the device, but the ch. 10 installment is the book servicing its own structure. Chapter 14, the wall's relocation ("the plywood stayed at Rendsburg, and the wall arrived entire") and chapter 15's "promoted to theater instrument": here the book audibly admires its own central prop. The wall is a great invention; by the fourth time a character tells me it's the most honest document in the headquarters, I'm being sold something I already bought. And the Aakjær exit-line — the coat left over the chair in chapter 1 is lovely; by his fifth over-the-shoulder curtain line (ch. 15, "Walls do not sign orders") it's a mannerism the book performs for its own satisfaction.

**4. UNDERSERVED.** I'd have paid for a full extra chapter of Rylski. His end — dictating his own filing dates while Kuragin builds the wall stone by stone, then returning to a map room with "no column for it" — is two pages doing the work of ten, and I wanted the ten. I wanted one more page of Merete, whose single letter (ch. 7) is the finest domestic writing in the book — the household's order of battle in schoolmistress hand — and whose door-step verdict in chapter 20 earns more scene than it gets. I wanted the inside of one civil column, once: the book prices the families in the Opel from the staff side with great scruple, and I accept the withholding as design, but at chapter 7's deferral of Columns 231 and 232 I'd have paid a page to ride in the car. And the sea war is entirely reported — the seventy-two hours against the embarkation complex (ch. 15) arrive as observables on a wall, which is doctrinally correct and slightly stingy.

**5. APPARATUS.** A map: **yes, emphatically.** First actively wanted at chapter 3, Lammers' six-places speech; wanted hardest at chapter 7 (the Friedrichstadt geometry) and chapter 12 (west shoulder versus Schlei shoulder, where the ambiguity actually degraded my reading of the book's most important error). The want faded around chapter 13 — once the war contracts to the narrows and the Danevirke, the book has taught its ground by repetition — but the first half overcharges the reader for the lack, and a single front-map would have refunded hours. A cast list: **no.** Function carries the names; I never once lost anyone. A calendar: **marginal no.** Wanted once, around chapters 10–12, when day-count and date drift apart; the book's own two-times discipline otherwise keeps the reader current — this is a novel that timestamps itself.

**6. THE VERDICT.** Good deal — clearly. Five to six hours of attention against goods I expect to keep for years, at a length (short) that respects the clock: the book never once padded on me, and my complaints above are about texture, not waste of volume. It is the rare war novel whose didactic freight is carried in-scene, paid for by named characters at posted rates, and its restraint — no Lübeck march, no winking, the war "closing" rather than ending — is adult in a way this genre almost never is. I'd recommend it to the reader who owns both Forester's *The Good Shepherd* and a shelf of staff-college history; to anyone who runs an organization on dashboards; and to the Clancy reader ready to graduate from hardware to arithmetic. I would not hand it to a reader who needs interiority, blood at ground level, or more than one woman with lines. The one-sentence warning: everyone in this book — Danish, German, Polish, Russian — speaks in the same burnished lapidary maxim, and the battles happen offstage, on paper; if you can't love a war told entirely through its receipts, this ledger will not open for you.</result>

# RESPONDENT A — Map Program for *The Mission*

## The thesis, in one line

The book already contains its own map. Chapter 1 opens with a German captain on a chair, taking nine days of chinagraph off the operations board with a razor blade until "the map of the Baltic Approaches emerged at 1:250,000, clean, the peninsula hanging down into Germany like something anatomical." **The map program is that base sheet, undressed** — the ground the corps fights over, with every mark the staff made on it withheld. The reader is handed the clean plexiglass and made to draw the war on it in their own head, chapter by chapter. That is the book's method (the wall shows inputs, not conclusions; the reader does the subtraction) turned onto its own apparatus. It is also, not coincidentally, the design that cannot spoil a single suspense mechanic, because it shows no war at all.

I commit to **two maps, both pure static geography, no front, no arrows, no dates, no enemy** — placed in the front matter as a findable pair.

---

## (1) How many maps, and what each shows

**Two.** Not one, not per-Part. The reviewer's instinct ("a single front-map would have refunded hours") is right about *timing* and wrong about *count*, because the pain occurs at two irreconcilable scales:

- **Map 1 — THE APPROACHES** (theater, ~1:1,000,000). The whole LANDJUT geography Aalborg→Rostock: the peninsula, both seas, the belts, the Kiel Canal, the transport skeleton down 400 km of Jutland. Serves **ch. 3** — Lammers' "one bridge pair, two ferry sets, one rail line single-track south of Vamdrup, four hundred kilometers of road."
- **Map 2 — THE NECK** (operating area, ~1:250,000 — the ch.-1 scale). The waist of the peninsula: Kiel Canal, Rendsburg, the Schlei, the Danevirke earthwork, the Eider valve, the Treene marshes, Sehestedt, Neumünster. Serves **ch. 7** (the three-crossing Eider complex) and **ch. 12** (Schlei shoulder vs. canal-west-of-Sehestedt).

A theater sheet physically cannot show that the Eider has three separate crossings a kilometre apart; a neck sheet cannot show a 400-km march corridor. One scale would fail one of the two crisis chapters. Two sheets, doing a **coarse→fine handoff**, is the honest minimum — and it stops there. The war "fades after ch. 13 once it contracts to the neck" (reviewer); the neck sheet already owns that ground, so no third map is earned.

Map 2 carries one small titled **detail-inset** in a corner: the Friedrichstadt / Sorgbrück rail swing-bridge / Nordfeld ferry cluster — three dots that collapse to one at neck scale but are the whole confusion of ch. 7. Pure geography (all three crossings existed in 1983); no spoiler.

## (2) Where each sits

**Both in the front matter, as a facing/sequential pair before chapter 1** — Map 1 then Map 2. Reasons, in order of force:

- **Findability.** The reader's want spikes at ch. 3 and again at 7 and 12. A reader thumbs to the *front* for a map by reflex; a part-opener buried at ch. 9 is invisible when you need it at ch. 7. Front matter is the one location reachable from every crisis.
- **The ch.-1 rhyme.** Meeting the clean 1:250,000 sheet in the front matter, then reading a razor blade reveal that exact sheet on page one, makes the apparatus and the prose the *same object*. The reader has already held what the captain uncovers.
- **No reprints, no part-openers.** Reprinting at each Part edges toward the per-Part scheme the reviewer explicitly did not want, and every reprint is a temptation to "update" the map with where the front now is — which is the spoiler. Static maps, printed once, cannot drift.

I reject **in-chapter maps** (they'd force a scale and a moment, i.e. a snapshot of the front = spoiler) and **endpapers** (a hardcover feature; this is a 5.5×8.5 trade paperback with no printed endpaper stock).

## (3) Visual register, and the argument

**A period NATO/German staff base sheet at ~1:250,000 — the JOG/TK-series look of the early 1980s — rendered monochrome and clean.** Not modern flat cartography, not hex-and-counter wargame dressing.

- Water open (hairline coastline, optional fine stipple); land plain; relief only where it is load-bearing — the **Danevirke as a hachured earthwork**, the **Treene/Eider marshes** as the standard marsh stipple. These two symbols do real work in ch. 12; nothing else needs relief.
- Roads by **line-weight and dash to encode the atlas's own classes** (motorway / trunk / federal), rail as the standard tick-line with **double vs. single tick** (Vamdrup, the Marschbahn) — because single-track is permanent 1983 infrastructure, not the campaign, and it is precisely what ch. 3 leans on. Ferries as dashed sea-routes. The IGB as a plain national frontier.
- Typography a plain period cartographic face, 1983 spellings (Aabenraa, Rødbyhavn, Nykøbing F., Korsør). A bar scale in km, a small north tick, graticule ticks at the neat-line. **No compass rose, no cartouche, no unit symbology.**

The argument is the thesis: the register should *be the object the characters work on*, stripped of their chinagraph. Monochrome is not a budget compromise dressed as a virtue — many real staff sheets were single-ink, it happens to match a 1-color trade interior, and it reinforces the book's austerity. Any color, any arrow, any flourish would be the "try-hard wargame dressing" the standing constraint forbids and the winking the register forbids.

## (4) What the maps deliberately REFUSE to show — and why

1. **The front line / FLOT — ever.** The war's shape stays in the prose. This is the suspense guarantee (a full campaign trace up front "could spoil the war's shape") *and* the thematic engine (the chinagraph is the reader's to imagine).
2. **Arrows / axes of advance.** Red's approach up from Lübeck, blue's two-shoulder counterstroke, the Great Belt ferry release of the Zealand brigades, the December pocket — every one lives in the back half. No arrow survives contact with the suspense clause.
3. **Formations / unit boxes / OOB symbology.** Unit positions *are* the campaign. Order of battle is the cast/OOB table's job — a separate apparatus, reference-only, already licensed as non-counting.
4. **Time.** No dates, no phase lines, no DTG. The map is timeless terrain. This also refuses the "LARP line DK drew at chapter DTGs" and the wargame-clock temptation in one stroke.
5. **The enemy as enemy.** Mecklenburg, the IGB, Rostock and the two legal crossings (Schlutup road, Herrnburg rail) appear as *geography and real infrastructure* — nothing is colored red, nothing is arrowed. The map is the ground, not the fight.

The refusal is the point: the maps carry **reference and record only, never the didactic payload.** *Why* the Little Belt pinch matters, *why* the chain is not the sum of its links — that stays in Lammers' mouth in ch. 3. The map only lets the reader put a finger on each noun.

## (5) Worked example — Map 1, THE APPROACHES, complete

**Frame.** Portrait plate (rotated to a landscape plate if the compositor finds the width tight — reader turns the book, standard for trade fiction). Neat-line rectangle; graticule ticks only, at 54°/55°/56°/57° N and 9°–12° E. Bar scale in km, small north tick, plain title block lower-right: **THE APPROACHES · NOVEMBER 1983**. A four-line legend — road (by class), rail (double/single), ferry route, frontier, corps HQ. Scale stated honestly (~1:1,000,000; wider than the ch.-1 sheet, because it must hold 400 km).

**Extent.** N: Aalborg (57.05°). S: Hamburg / Rostock (~53.5°). W: the North Sea coast and Husum. E: Rostock–Wismar and, across the belts, København. The reader sees Jutland, Funen, Zealand, Lolland-Falster, Schleswig-Holstein, Hamburg, and just enough of the Mecklenburg coast — never the GDR interior, which would begin to imply the enemy's rear.

**Labeled features.**
- *Waters:* Nordsee, Ostsee, Little Belt, Great Belt, Fehmarn Belt, Kiel Canal, the Schlei and Eider as thin lines.
- *Regions (light):* Jylland, Fyn, Sjælland, Lolland-Falster, Schleswig-Holstein, Mecklenburg.
- *Settlements (atlas nodes, 1983 spelling):* the E3 chain Aalborg–Aarhus–Vejle–Fredericia–Kolding–Haderslev–Aabenraa–Padborg; Middelfart–Odense–Nyborg; Korsør–Slagelse–Ringsted–København; Nykøbing F.–Rødbyhavn; Flensburg–Schleswig–**Rendsburg**–Neumünster–Kiel–Eckernförde; Husum–Friedrichstadt–Heide–Itzehoe; Lübeck–Oldenburg–Puttgarden; Hamburg; Rostock–Wismar–Schwerin. **Rendsburg** carries a single quiet HQ tick — the book is told from that room; that is record, not campaign.
- *The transport skeleton (the ch.-3 payload):* the E3/A10 spine down Jutland; the **Little Belt bridge pair** (the only fixed pinch Funen↔Jutland); the **Great Belt drawn as a dashed ferry route** (the atlas absence enforces: there is no 1983 bridge); the **Storstrøm** single fixed link off Zealand; the **Vogelfluglinie** ferry Rødby–Puttgarden; A7/E3 Flensburg–Rendsburg–Neumünster–Hamburg; the west-coast B5/Marschbahn; rail as tick-lines with the double→single change **at Vamdrup**; the IGB with Schlutup and Herrnburg as real crossings.

Every one of Lammers' "six places" is on this sheet as named infrastructure.

**What the eye does at ch. 3.** Lammers names a list of nouns — bridge pair, ferry sets, Vamdrup, four hundred kilometers. Without a map that is vocabulary. With Map 1 the reader flips to the front, runs the E3 down the length of Jutland, sees the Little Belt pair as the *sole* fixed pinch, finds Vamdrup where the double tick goes single, reads the ferries as dashed gaps in the line — and lays the pinch points onto the route *himself*. "A chain, not stages" stops being an aphorism and becomes a thing the eye traces. The list becomes a road.

**What the eye does at ch. 12.** Now the war is at the neck, and the reader returns to Map 1 for a different job: to place the neck in the whole. He sees the Kiel Canal as the peninsula's waist, Rendsburg on it, the **Schlei biting in from the Baltic (east)** and the **Eider/Treene from the North Sea (west)** — sea on both flanks, nowhere left to give. The chapter's fatal ambiguity, "west shoulder versus east shoulder," resolves at coarse grain: *west = the Treene/marsh flank, east = the Schlei flank.* Map 1 gives that coarse fix; the reader then drops to **Map 2** for Sehestedt, the Danevirke, and the exact crossings. The two maps handing off coarse→fine is the honest reason there are two of them — and it is exactly the resolution ch. 12 was starving for.

## (6) Production path

**Data.** The geography is already audited and queryable: `atlas/data/theater-1983.toml` — nodes with real lat/lon, edges tagged by mode/class/route/km/crossing, an enforced **absences** table (no Great Belt bridge, no Fehmarn link, the sealed Gadebusch–Ratzeburg axis), and the whole set period-verified in `reference/transport-1983.md`. Route designations are 1983-correct (E3 not E45, E66 not E20, F-numbers east of the wire). This is the authoritative network topology; nothing on the map's road/rail/ferry layer is invented or remembered.

**Base layer (redistributability-safe).** CLAUDE.md requires the repo stay freely redistributable and forbids holdings content leaking into build outputs. So the cartographic base is **public-domain only**: Natural Earth (CC0) coastline, land, rivers at this scale; the Kiel Canal, the **Danevirke earthwork trace**, the marsh polygons, and the Eider valve crossings hand-digitized from PD/CC0 sources. No purchased or scanned basemap touches the build. (The atlas's own note flags lat/lon as display-tier, km as authoritative — so the *base* geometry is anchored to Natural Earth's true coastline, and the atlas network is drawn as an audited route-diagram over it, which is honestly how a 1:1,000,000 route sheet reads anyway.)

**Tooling (reproducible geometry, hand-finished register).**
- Add a small exporter to the existing atlas CLI — `python3 -m atlas geojson` (~40 lines beside the current `path`/`flow`/`check` commands) — emitting nodes and edges as GeoJSON with their class/mode/crossing attributes. The map then *regenerates from corrected data*, consistent with the repo's "geography is a queryable input, not latent prose memory" ethos and the "measure before asserting" standing memory.
- Style the GeoJSON + Natural Earth base in **QGIS** (or a scripted matplotlib/GeoPandas render for the reproducible geometry), then a **cartographer/book-designer does the period-register pass in Illustrator/Inkscape** — the earthwork hachure, marsh stipple, label placement, the 1983 face. Software makes the geometry true; a human makes it look like a 1983 sheet. Export **press-ready vector (PDF/EPS)**.
- **Proof gate:** run the two plates past the doctrine auditor / veteran reader / period auditor before lock, exactly as the manuscript's other apparatus is certified. The atlas's absences table is the automatic guard against the specimen's original sins (the invented Great Belt bridge, the wrong border) reappearing on the map.

**Print spec.** Trade paperback, 5.5×8.5in trim. Design **monochrome (1-color black)** from the first stroke — line-weight and dash carry class, so nothing depends on color; this matches a 1-ink interior *and* the authentic period sheet. Pure vector base (crisp at any size); if a hillshade is ever wanted it stays out — it muddies at 1-ink and the book needs relief in only two places, both better served by symbol than by shade. Each map to a full page inside the existing live-area/margins of `build/the-mission.pdf`; theater plate rotated to landscape if width demands. Two plates, front matter, printed once.

---

**Summary of the commitment.** Two monochrome period base sheets — THE APPROACHES (theater) and THE NECK (operating area) — in the front matter, drawn from the audited atlas over a public-domain base, showing the ground and nothing the war does to it. They refund ch. 3, 7, and 12 by naming geography, refuse every campaign mark that could spoil the back half or carry didactic payload, and make the apparatus the same clean 1:250,000 sheet the razor blade reveals on page one. The reader draws the chinagraph. That was always the book's method.</result>

# RESPONDENT C — The Map Program for *The Mission*

## The thesis, stated once

This book's whole method is a wall that shows inputs and refuses to show movement — the boards are green while the division isn't there; the war is the thing the instruments can't see. **The map program must be built of the same refusal.** Two maps, both pure terrain-and-network, both showing the ground the corps holds and *nothing the corps decides*. No front line, no arrow, no phase line, no date, ever. The suspense the RFP guards is protected not by hiding geography but by declining to draw the war onto it — which is not a compromise with the book's idiom, it *is* the book's idiom rendered as an object. The reader is handed the same base sheet Roloff pinned up before he started inking, and told: here is the ground; the war is what you can't yet see on it. That is the two-columns ledger made spatial.

Everything below commits to that.

---

## (1) How many maps, and what each shows — TWO

**Map 1 — "The Baltic Approaches."** The whole board: north Jutland down to the Elbe, the North Sea coast across to Zealand and the Mecklenburg shore. Its subject is *the transport network* — because the network is what ch. 3 is about and what the first half's suspense runs on. It is drawn so that the six chokepoints of Lammers' speech are the map's most legible features, shown **as geometry, never as numbers**: the Little Belt bridge *pair* (the only fixed Jutland→Funen link, drawn as a visible pinch); the Great Belt as a *gap* crossed only by a dashed ferry line (no bridge — the atlas's founding correction, made visible); the rail line drawn *double north of Vamdrup, single south of it*, a hairline the eye catches; the Storstrøm as the single thread off Zealand; the Kiel Canal as a bold water-line across the neck with its handful of crossings; and the IGB east of Lübeck with exactly two gaps in it — Schlutup road, Herrnburg rail — red's two-crossing neck as a fact of the drawing.

**Map 2 — "The Neck."** Large-scale, the peninsula's waist: the Kiel Canal, Rendsburg, Sehestedt, the Schlei and its head, the Danevirke rampart, Schleswig, the Eider–Treene–Friedrichstadt valve, the Treene marshes, Eckernförde/Schwansen, Neumünster. This is the terrain of chs. 7, 8, 12, 13, 19, 19a — the west shoulder (Treene/canal) and the east shoulder (Schlei) legible *as ground*, so that when ch. 12's fires go the wrong way the reader can see which way was wrong.

Two scales because the reviewer's pain is at two scales and no single sheet serves both: theater-scale can't resolve Sehestedt-vs-Schlei; neck-scale can't hold the Great Belt ferry. The reviewer's "a single front-map would have refunded hours" names the *felt* need — one place to look, at no cost — not the prescription. I honor it by co-locating both (below), so consultation costs one flip.

---

## (2) Where each sits

- **Front matter, as a facing pair** immediately before Part I: Map 1 on the verso, Map 2 on the recto, so the open book shows both at once. Findable "at the front," re-findable forever, the way a reader learns where a novel's map lives and returns there.
- **Map 2 reprints once, as the Part III opener.** Parts II–III are entirely neck battles, and by the pocket-breakout (19a — the enemy coming up Roloff's street at Schleswig-Friedrichsberg, through the Missunde crossings) the reader is a long thumb-span from the front matter. A reprint is free art and pure refund. Map 1 does *not* reprint: the evidence says the need fades after ch. 13 as the war contracts to the neck — Map 1's job is the first half, and it ends there honestly.
- **Not endpapers** — a trade paperback has none (that's a case-bound feature); proposing them would be format-illiterate. A future hardback promotes the front-matter spread to endpapers unchanged.
- **Never in-chapter.** A figure mid-scene is a textbook, not a novel, and an in-chapter map invites the one annotation the program forbids (*this* scene's front line). Maps stay reference apparatus — consulted at will, narrating nothing. This is exactly the apparatus rule: reference and record, never payload.

---

## (3) Visual register — the base sheet off Roloff's own board

**Committed: a restrained 1983 staff base map — the clean printed base the wall's overlays were inked *onto* — monochrome line on cream, not modern trade cartography and not APP-6 wargame dress.**

The argument is the book's argument. Rahn arrives carrying "one folded map, 1:250,000, Baltic Approaches, 1961 printing, privately annotated" and reads it "the way other men read a will." Roloff inks overlays by hand; the war lives in grease pencil on plexiglass *above* a fixed base. The reader's map should be that base — the sheet before the grease pencil. Clean modern cartography would break register (too slick, an outside hand); full unit symbology would break two constraints at once (it *is* try-hard wargame dressing, and unit dispositions *are* the campaign trace the RFP forbids up front). The monochrome-line period base does thematic work for free: it is literally the thing the corps cannot annotate its way out of — the ground is knowable and fixed; the war is not. Water hatched, marsh stippled, the transport net as the map's spine, place-names in a period map face. On Map 2 the Danevirke goes in as an antiquarian dashed-rampart symbol — the thousand-year wall already on the sheet, facing south, before anyone alive drew a line near it.

---

## (4) What the maps deliberately REFUSE to show

- **No front lines, phase lines, arrows, boundaries, unit symbols, or dates.** Three reasons, each sufficient: (a) a campaign trace up front spoils the war's shape — the explicit constraint; (b) the wall's doctrine is that movement is the invisible column, so a map with arrows would be the book betraying its own instrument; (c) operational graphics would make the apparatus carry the didactic payload it is forbidden to carry. The reader draws the arrow in his own head — which is the method.
- **No capacities or tonnages printed on the sheet.** "27,500 t/d" is wargame dressing and belongs in the annex; the chokepoint is shown *as a pinch in the line*, so the reader sees the bottleneck without a number.
- **No fictional operational furniture** — the guidance lines, the SPERBER trace, the salient. Those are the war; the war stays off the base.
- **No red interior beyond the start-state geography** the text already establishes (the two IGB crossings, Rostock/Wismar/Bad Kleinen). Showing where red must physically come through is start conditions, not a reveal — the war's *shape* (the contraction, the counterstroke, the pocket) is nowhere visible in fixed terrain.

The refusal is the point: everything the maps won't draw is the book.

---

## (5) Worked example — Map 1 complete

**Frame &amp; extent.** North up. Top edge at Aalborg; bottom edge at Hamburg/the Elbe. Left edge the North Sea coast at Husum; right edge Rostock and the Mecklenburg shore. Discreet km scale bar, period style. Monochrome black line on cream; one optional muted second ink for water only if the interior build supports duotone (design assumes 1-color and degrades gracefully).

**Labeled features** (only what the text names or needs). Regions faint: JUTLAND, FUNEN, ZEALAND, LOLLAND-FALSTER, SCHLESWIG-HOLSTEIN, MECKLENBURG; seas named at the belts. Dots, hand-set: Aalborg · Aarhus · Vejle · Fredericia · Kolding · **Vamdrup** · Padborg · Flensburg · Schleswig · **RENDSBURG** (the one distinguishing mark on the whole sheet — a ringed dot, the HQ, the only concession to the military) · Neumünster · Kiel · Eckernförde · Husum · Friedrichstadt · Heide · Itzehoe · Hamburg · Lübeck · Middelfart · Odense · Nyborg · Korsør · Slagelse · Ringsted · København · Vordingborg · Nykøbing F · Rødby · Puttgarden · Oldenburg; across the wire, Ratzeburg · Schwerin · Wismar · Rostock · Bad Kleinen. The transport net carries the six throats by drawing alone: Little Belt bridge-pair pinch; Great Belt dashed ferry gap; the Vamdrup double→single hairline; the lone Storstrøm thread; the bold Kiel Canal with its counted crossings; the IGB fortified hatch with exactly two gaps. DK/DE border a light dashed political line. No front. No arrow. No date.

**What the eye does at ch. 3.** Lammers says the division's fate is decided by "perhaps six places — the bridge pair at the Belt, the ferry berths, Vamdrup where the rail goes to single track, and the crossings where their routes meet everything else." The reader flips to Map 1 and those six places *are already the sheet's most legible features* — the pinches are drawn as pinches. The speech stops being a list and becomes a route with visible throats: a division threading the bridge-pair, queuing at ferry gaps, choking at the Vamdrup hairline, four hundred kilometers down to the canal. The abstract chain becomes a picture, and — crucially — the reader supplies the motion himself. Hours refunded, exactly as predicted, without a single arrow.

**What the eye does at ch. 12.** The reader flips to Map 1 for the frame before going to Map 2 for the shoulders — and the same untouched sheet now reads inside-out. At ch. 3 the neck was "where the mobilization route ends." At ch. 12 the reader sees, at a glance, that the neck is the narrowest point on the entire board — canal, Schlei, and Treene marshes forming one natural wall — and that *behind it there is nothing but Jutland and the belts, i.e. everything*. The map explains why losing the narrows loses the war, and it does so having changed not one line since page one. The terrain held still; the reader's knowledge moved — the book's two-columns idea, delivered by the apparatus without the apparatus ever teaching. That re-reading is the whole return on putting the map up front and refusing to annotate it.

---

## (6) Production path

**Data — the map is a rendering of the atlas, not a fresh drawing.** This is the program's strongest guarantee. `atlas/data/theater-1983.toml` already holds the network as validated, *tested* data: node coordinates (lat/lon), edges tagged by mode/class/crossing, and an **absences table enforced by tests** — the Great Belt fixed link and the Fehmarn link *cannot* appear because the test suite fails if they do. The single-track-south-of-Vamdrup fact is in the `rail-fredericia-padborg` edge notes; the two-crossing IGB min-cut is in the data; the Kiel Canal crossings are enumerated. Drawing the map from this model, rather than from memory, retires the exact error class the atlas was built to kill (the wrong-border opening, the invented bridge). **The atlas's tests become the map's proofreader.**

**Tooling — committed path:**
1. Extend `atlas/` with a `render` command that emits SVG from the TOML: nodes placed by lat/lon (equirectangular is fine at 54°N; a light conic if we want it honest), edges styled by mode/class, crossings marked, absences omitted *by construction*. This is a small, in-repo, testable step and it keeps the network layer canonical.
2. Coastline/water/marsh polygons are **not** in the atlas (it's a graph, not geography), so they come from a **public-domain base — Natural Earth** at theater scale; the neck-scale Schlei/Eider/canal/Treene detail hand-traced from open sources. **Redistributability flag (repo holdings discipline):** base data must be PD or open-licensed and freely redistributable — no copyrighted map scans traced into the book; Natural Earth is PD, OSM is ODbL-with-attribution, hand-drawing is clean. This constraint is real for this repo and I'm naming it rather than assuming.
3. One styling pass — an illustrator, or a disciplined Inkscape/QGIS operator — lays the period register (line-weight hierarchy, hand-set labels, water hatch, marsh stipple, the Danevirke rampart symbol on Map 2) over the SVG + base. Map 2's few fictional/annotated features (Sehestedt, the Danevirke trace) get hand-lettered accents to signal "the staff's own additions," against a machine-clean base — the overlay/base distinction made visible.
4. Add the two nodes the network graph lacks but the neck map needs (Sehestedt; confirm Friedrichstadt/Schleswig/Eckernförde present — they are). Trace the Danevirke from `reference/danevirke.md` (Schlei/Hedeby → Treene marshes, ~19 km).

**Print spec.** Trade 5.5×8.5, interior almost certainly 1-color (color interior blows POD economics and the monochrome register wants black-on-cream anyway). Vector throughout (SVG→PDF, placed as full-page figures in the existing pandoc/LaTeX build behind `build/the-mission.pdf`); 300+ dpi if any raster fallback. **Line hierarchy tuned for the reduction, proofed at trim, not on screen** — at 5.5" the theater map is dense, so bold-canal / medium-coast / hairline-secondary must survive downsampling. Front-matter pair may bleed to trim; the Part III reprint sits in the live area. One period-plausible label face across both.

**Sequence to a finished object:** write the `render` pass (in-repo, tested against the absences) → drop the PD base → one styling pass per map → proof both at trim in the real build → iterate the neck map's hand features against `outline` §4 and `danevirke.md`. The data is already canon and already defended by tests; the remaining work is a renderer and a styling hand, not a research effort.

---

**In one line:** two monochrome staff base-sheets, drawn from the tested atlas, co-located up front and reprinted once at Part III, showing all the ground and none of the war — the wall's refusal turned into the reader's own instrument.</result>
