# RESPONDENT D — The map program for *The Mission*

## The thesis I'm committing to

The book opens (ch. 1, ENDEX) and closes (ch. 20, coda) on **the same object: a clean map, razored clean.** Between those two clean states, a war is fought across it in grease pencil, chinagraph, and plexiglass overlays — every one of which gets wiped. The book's whole discipline is that the *clean map is reference and record*; the marks on it are the argument, and the argument lives in the prose, earned in-scene.

So the reader's map is the corps' map with nothing on it. **The apparatus gives the reader the clean plate the staff starts from; the war happens on it in the reader's head, exactly as it happens on the wall — and gets wiped, exactly as on the wall.** This single idea resolves all four standing constraints at once: it refunds the reviewer's hours, it carries reference-only (never the payload), it cannot spoil a campaign it deliberately never draws, and its register is not "staff-map cosplay" — it *is* the one map the book itself venerates.

Everything below follows from that.

## (1) How many maps, and what each shows

**Two maps, one geography at two scales — a nested reference pair, not a per-Part series.**

- **Map 1 — the theater plate ("The Board").** The full LANDJUT operating area: the peninsula's neck, the two seas, the Kiel Canal as its spine, the ~24 named places, the transport skeleton (road / rail / ferry, with the water-crossings marked), the Danish border at Padborg, and the inner-German border SE of Lübeck. This is the standing reference the reader flips back to. It answers ch. 3 outright and stages ch. 12.
- **Map 2 — the neck detail ("The Narrows").** Larger scale on the peninsula's waist only: Rendsburg on the canal, Sehestedt east of it, the Schlei reaching in from the Baltic, the Treene marshes and the Eider to the west, the Danevirke earthwork across the gap, and — at *this* scale and only this scale — the Friedrichstadt crossing complex resolved into its three real crossings (road bridge; rail swing-bridge ~1 km west; Nordfeld heavy ferry). This is the ch. 7 map and the ch. 12 map.

**Why two, against the reader-ledger's round-1 "one early front map."** There is a hard scale conflict the single-plate answer can't survive. Ch. 3's six chokepoints span from the Little Belt down the whole E3 corridor to the Eider — that demands a *wide* extent. Ch. 7's crisis is three crossings within a kilometre of each other; ch. 12's hinge is the ~15 km relationship of Sehestedt, the Schlei head, and the Treene marshes. A plate zoomed out far enough to hold Lammers' six chokepoints physically cannot resolve Friedrichstadt's three. R1 was right that the answer is *not* per-Part (that program leaks the campaign's shape); it was under-specified in calling for one plate. Two scales of one timeless geography is the minimal honest answer — and it reads as *one* reference (a facing spread you consult), not as chapter-by-chapter front maps.

If production economics force exactly one, the theater plate survives and the neck degrades to a boxed inset in its lower corner. But my committed recommendation is the pair.

## (2) Where each sits

**Front matter, as a facing reference spread, immediately before Chapter 1** — after the title and the "How this book was made" page.

Not endpapers: a trade paperback is perfect-bound and has no pastedowns, so "endpaper maps" aren't available to this build (a production reality worth stating rather than wishing away). Not part-openers: part-openers are where a per-Part program would live, and that program is refused (§4). Not in-chapter: an in-chapter map at ch. 7 or ch. 12 would (a) tie the apparatus to a scene and risk reading as illustration/didactic aid, and (b) arrive *after* the ch. 3 debt is already owed. The reviewer's own datum is decisive — the *first* want is ch. 3, so the map must be in hand before ch. 3, which means front matter.

Each map gets its own page (live area ~4×7" inside the 0.75" margins); **neither crosses the gutter**, because perfect-binding eats gutter-crossing detail. Theater plate on the recto the reader is most likely to dog-ear; neck detail facing or overleaf.

## (3) Visual register, with the argument

**The book's own idiom — which resolves to the period staff base-map, because the book already made that its central object.** This is not a third choice between "period staff aesthetic" and "clean modern cartography"; the book has already collapsed the two.

Concretely:
- **The map is drawn clean — the plate *before* the grease pencil.** No operational graphics of any kind. This is literally "the clean map razored clean" of chs. 1 and 20, handed to the reader.
- **One ink.** Body-text black line art, water rendered as an open or finely-hatched field. No red/blue. Colour-coding the two sides is doing the chinagraph's job — it's the exact "wargame dressing" the constraint forbids, and it would smuggle in force geometry the vantage refuses. One-colour interior line art is also the trade-book norm and keeps the print honest.
- **Typography married to the text.** Labels set in the book's own face (TeX Gyre Pagella, already the body font) — towns in roman, waters in small caps, the restrained staff-map convention. The map must read as a *page of the book*, not a bolt-on plate. This single move does more for register than any amount of styling: a map lettered in the body font is felt as continuous with the prose that venerates it.
- **Hand-feel, machine-truth.** Generalised coastline (the 1:500k "atlas-from-memory tier" the atlas itself works at), but geometrically faithful. It should look like a competent staff officer's base map, not a satellite tile and not a decorative antique.

The argument in one line: the register question answers itself once you notice the book's first and last images are both this exact object. We are not choosing an aesthetic *for* the book; we are printing the book's own recurring image and letting the reader hold it.

## (4) What the maps deliberately REFUSE to show — and why

1. **No front line, no FEBA/FLOT, no axis-of-advance arrows, no phase lines, no dates.** This is the load-bearing refusal. It protects the suspense mechanic (the campaign's *shape* — war arriving from the Lübeck SE and contracting NW to the neck, the close-not-end ending — is the book's engine; a front trace up front spoils it), it keeps the apparatus to reference-only (a trace *is* didactic payload), and it keeps the register clean of wargame graphics. The war is drawn nowhere; it is *narrated*.
2. **No red/blue, no unit symbols, no strengths, no OOB.** Sides and forces are the grease pencil's and the (separately-licensed) cast/OOB table's business. The map is terrain, not order of battle.
3. **No corps/sector boundaries, no rear-boundary, no LANDJUT limits** — only Rendsburg marked, as a small square, because the reader must know where they are standing (the entire book is the view *from* Rendsburg). Boundaries are operational graphics and mildly leak force geometry.
4. **The map is as blind to red's rear as the corps is.** Rostock, Wismar, Schwerin appear only as edge-of-world names past the IGB; no assembly areas, no depth. The corps only ever sees red through handsets, and the cartography honours that vantage — a refusal that is also a characterisation of the whole book.
5. **The map does not label its own chokepoints.** It shows the network truthfully — the bridge *pair* at the Little Belt as two lines, the rail as double-then-single with the tick falling at Vamdrup, ferries as dashes — and lets the reader *count Lammers' six off the map as he counts them off in speech.* The map editorialises nothing; the reader earns the chokepoints exactly as Holt does. This is the "earned in-scene" discipline applied to cartography: annotate the constraints and you've briefed the reader; draw the network honestly and the constraints are *discoverable*, which is the book's entire method.
6. **No time / no clock on the map.** The war-calendar question (R1: marginal-no) is separate and stays separate.

## (5) Worked example — Map 1, the theater plate, complete

**Frame and extent.** Portrait, filling the 4×7" live area. North edge at the Little Belt (Fredericia / Middelfart) with a corner of Funen and the Great Belt ferry track and Korsør reaching into the top-right (Aakjær's Zealand, and the ferry the reinforcement rides). South edge at Hamburg. West edge the North Sea coast (Husum, the Eider mouth, the Marschbahn). East edge the Baltic — Kieler Bucht, the Schlei, Fehmarn. Lower-right corner: the inner-German border SE of Lübeck, with Rostock/Wismar/Schwerin as terminal names. The **Kiel Canal is drawn boldest of all lines**, the one unmistakable east–west stroke across the middle of the sheet, because it is the peninsula's structural fact and the book's recurring hinge.

**Labelled features.**
- *Waters (small caps):* North Sea, Kieler Bucht, Little Belt, Great Belt, the Schlei, the Eider, the Trave, and the Kiel Canal.
- *The two borders:* the Danish frontier at Padborg/Ellund (a light dashed political line); the IGB SE of Lübeck drawn as the one heavier fortified line on the sheet — period-honest, because it *was* a physical fact on every 1983 map, not a war-drawn mark.
- *Places (dot + roman):* Fredericia, Kolding, Vamdrup, Haderslev, Aabenraa, Padborg, Flensburg, Schleswig, **Rendsburg (HQ square)**, Neumünster, Kiel, Eckernförde, Husum, Friedrichstadt, Heide, Itzehoe, Lübeck, Oldenburg, Puttgarden, Ratzeburg, Hamburg; across the water Nyborg, Korsør; past the wire Schwerin, Wismar, Rostock.
- *Network:* the E3/A7 spine; rail drawn with the double/single distinction visible so **the tick where double becomes single falls at Vamdrup**; ferries as dashed tracks (Great Belt Korsør–Nyborg; the Vogelfluglinie). Crossings marked as small gaps/symbols where line meets water: the **Little Belt bridge pair**, the canal crossings at Rendsburg and the Rader Hochbrücke, the Eider bridge at Friedrichstadt.

**What the reader's eye does at ch. 3.** Lammers: *"one bridge pair, two ferry sets, one rail line single-track south of Vamdrup, and four hundred kilometres of road."* The eye traces the Jyske Division's march down the plate — Fredericia, *across the Little Belt* (there is the bridge pair, two lines), down the E3, *there is Vamdrup and the tick* where the rail thins, on to Schleswig and the Eider. The reader counts the six off the map as Lammers counts them off in speech. Every noun in the war's "first honest sentence" acquires a place the eye can land on. The ninety-hours arithmetic stops being assertion and becomes a chain the reader can *see* is only as strong as its links — which is the chapter's thesis, delivered by the reader's own eye instead of by narration.

**What the reader's eye does at ch. 12 — the same plate, opposite use.** Now the reader is not tracing a route; they are orienting a battle whose hinge is west-shoulder-versus-east-shoulder — the exact ambiguity the reviewer says degraded the reading. The eye goes to the neck: finds **Rendsburg on the canal**, finds **Sehestedt** east of it (the forced crossing the mass drives out of), finds the **Schlei** reaching in from the Baltic (the eastern shoulder — the main effort), finds the **Treene marshes** west (the western shoulder — where the brigade is visibly breaking, where the fires get wrongly weighted). The "narrows" resolves into the physical gap between the Schlei's head and the marshes. "Fires weighted west, the blow came east, ninety minutes to re-lay across the neck" converts from a verbal muddle into a spatial fact the reader holds effortlessly. And this is the payoff worth naming: **the reader uses the identical clean plate first as a schedule and then as a battlefield** — which is precisely the book's own move, the same wall read first as intake curves and then as a war. The map's dual use rhymes with the book's argument. (The finest ch. 12 work — Sehestedt-to-Schlei geometry — is legible here; the ch. 7 three-crossing close-up is where Map 2 takes over.)

## (6) Production path

**Data.** The atlas is the source. `atlas/data/theater-1983.toml` already carries every node with lat/lon and every edge with mode / class / route / crossing tags and *period-verified* designations (E3 not E45, the Vamdrup single-track, ferries-not-bridges). The map is the atlas made visible — and it inherits the atlas's **absence tests** (no Great Belt fixed link, no Farø, no Fehmarn link, the IGB sealed at Gadebusch), so the specimen's whole error class is *untypable in the cartography* exactly as it is in the prose. The atlas already declares itself "source of truth for geography"; drawing from it keeps that contract.

**Base geography.** The atlas gives network topology, not coastlines. Take the timeless land/water base (coastline, the Schlei's shape, the Eider, the canal alignment) from a **public-domain source — Natural Earth 10m coast/rivers**, clipped and generalised. This matters for more than convenience: the repo is public and must stay freely redistributable, so a scanned period 1:500k sheet would be a copyright/holdings problem — the precise thing CLAUDE.md's holdings discipline exists to prevent. Clean separation of layers guarantees no anachronism leaks: the *modern PD basemap* supplies only timeless coast and water; the *atlas* supplies the entire datable network, so a 1998 bridge on the modern tile can never reach the plate because the network layer is drawn from 1983 edges only.

**Tooling.** A small generator in `atlas/` (the package is Python 3.14 already) reads the TOML and emits **SVG** — a purpose-built writer, not matplotlib (which is ugly and fights label placement). SVG → PDF for inclusion. The one thing that can't be automated is label collision; that is a hand pass. Division of labour: the model drafts the generator and hand-tunes labels; **DK rules the aesthetic** (per the project's own "human diagnoses, instrument executes" discipline). Add `make maps` (atlas → SVG → PDF) and drop the two PDFs into the pandoc `FRONT_MATTER` sequence — where they are already excluded from the narrative wordcount by the existing build (apparatus, per the 2026-07-23 ruling).

**Print spec.** Vector throughout, so resolution-independent; 1-colour black line art, water as open/hatched field — matched to the 5.5×8.5, 0.75" margins, Pagella labels. For the eventual print house: supply vector PDF (fallback 1200 dpi bitmap). No bleed, no gutter-crossing art. Cost stays at the ordinary interior-line-art tier — no colour plate, no coated-stock insert — which is the register-honest choice as well as the cheap one.

---

**In one sentence:** print the clean plate the book already worships — the whole board at theater scale and the neck at battle scale, both timeless, both one-ink, both lettered in the book's own hand — drawn from the atlas so it cannot lie about 1983 and refusing every grease-pencil mark so it cannot spoil the war, and the reviewer gets their refunded hours without the apparatus ever carrying a gram of the payload.</result>

# RESPONDENT B — The Map Program for *The Mission*

## The one idea everything hangs on

The book's first chapter opens on a map **razored clean** — the acetate the corps starts each morning with, before anyone grease-pencils the war onto it. That clean base overlay is not a metaphor to decorate; it is the whole program. **The reader gets the same blank instrument the staff gets, and fills it from the prose exactly as the staff fills theirs from reports.**

So my maps show *the ground and the fixed network, and nothing that moves.* No front line, no unit, no arrow, no date. This is not timidity about spoilers — it is the book's epistemology made physical. A front-trace map would hand the reader the very thing the novel's method is built to withhold: the war's shape, which is supposed to be *assembled*, not *issued*. The apparatus rule ("reference and record only, never the didactic payload") and the suspense constraint ("a full campaign trace up front could spoil the war's shape") are the same rule, and the clean overlay satisfies both by construction. The map cannot spoil the war because the war is never on it.

## (1) How many maps, and what each shows — **TWO**

The reviewer's page-located evidence resolves to two wants at two scales, and honoring it means two sheets — not the per-Part maps the outline drafted (rightly rejected: three operational fronts *are* a campaign trace), and not the single sheet the reviewer named as a floor. "One front map would have refunded hours" is the minimum that stops the bleeding; the disciplined maximum that serves all three pain points at legible scale is two. Not five, not per-chapter.

- **Map 1 — THE APPROACHES** (theater / the ninety-hours corridor). The Jutland peninsula and the western approaches to the isles: Aalborg down to Hamburg, the two Belts to the east. Serves **ch. 3** — Lammers' speech about a division strung along four hundred kilometres of road, held hostage by "perhaps six places." Every one of those six sits on this sheet.
- **Map 2 — THE NECK** (operational-tactical / the waist of the peninsula). Husum to Eckernförde, the Kiel Canal up to Schleswig and the Danevirke. Serves **ch. 7** (the Eider valve) and **ch. 12** (the narrows). This is the workhorse: it also carries four of ch. 3's six chokepoints (the meeting-crossings), and because the back-half war *contracts onto exactly this ground*, it keeps paying off silently through chs. 13–19 — which is why the reviewer's "fades after ch. 13" holds without a third map.

The two frames **overlap deliberately at Rendsburg-on-the-canal** — the southern margin of Map 1 is the middle of Map 2 — so a reader hands off from one to the other on a shared landmark, the way you register two staff overlays by a common tick. And the sequence itself does quiet work: **theater → neck is a zoom that mirrors the campaign's own contraction from a peninsula-wide race to a fight over one earthwork** — achieving thematically what a front-trace would achieve literally, at zero spoiler cost.

## (2) Where each sits — **front matter, as recto plates, shown once**

Both maps go in the front matter after the title/copyright leaf, Map 1 then Map 2, with **Map 2 on the recto immediately facing the opening of Chapter 1** (the last thing seen before reading; also the map the book earns hardest).

I reject the other two placements on the record:

- **Not endpapers.** This is a perfect-bound trade paperback (`build/the-mission.pdf`, 5.5×8.5). Paperbacks have no endpapers — that is a case-bound feature. The reading build is a single pandoc→xelatex PDF with no cover pipeline, so "endpaper" here can only mean a front-matter page. Call the thing by its true production home.
- **Not in-chapter / facing the chapter of need.** In-chapter maps (a) would force the neck map to repeat at 7 *and* 12; (b) read as textbook plates and break the novelistic register; (c) are fragile under pandoc's float placement. Front matter is where a reader of *any* mapped novel knows to look, keeps the maps as *findable reference* rather than *interrupting narration*, and lets the same sheet be re-consulted at 7 and again at 12.

## (3) Visual register — **the clean base overlay, inked by the headquarters' own hand**

Not a period NATO staff map (that path needs APP-6 symbology we're refusing, and lands squarely in the "try-hard wargame dressing" the RFP forbids). Not clean modern GIS cartography (hillshade and sans-serif read as a tourist atlas — anachronistic in feel, wrong register). **The book's own idiom**, which is specific and available: Roloff at the plans desk *inking overlays*; the wall built by hand; truth drawn "in a steady hand."

Concretely:
- **Monochrome, printed in the same black as the text** — so the maps read as body of the book, not inserted plates from another production. One tonal fill only: water as a pale flat tint or fine stipple.
- **Fine single-weight coastlines; the canal a firm double line; rail as the cartographer's ladder-hatch; roads thin.** The **Danevirke rendered as a period earthwork hachure** — the single antique flourish, *earned* because it is a thousand-year rampart, not a 1983 feature.
- **Place names in small caps set in the book's own face (TeX Gyre Pagella).** A plain `N`, a plain scale bar. No compass rose, no legend block, **no grid squares** — a grid is a staff overlay, and an overlay is the war.

The argument for this register is not taste; it is that **refusing to draw the war on the map is the same gesture as the book refusing to narrate the war except through the wall.** The reader holds the clean instrument and annotates it mentally from the prose. The emptiness is the method.

Period stance, stated once: the maps are drawn *now, for the reader,* but obey **1983 truth** — no Great Belt bridge, Vamdrup single-track transition marked, E3 (never E45), the Christiansfeld–Skovby motorway gap open. Period-truthful, not period-pastiche. That line is the register's spine.

## (4) What the maps deliberately REFUSE, and why

1. **Any front line / FLOT / trace of contact.** It is the payload the prose delivers and the suspense the book sells. This is the entire point.
2. **Unit symbols / OOB / echelon boxes.** The disposition is what the reader assembles from the wall; drawing it pre-empts the method and dates the map to one hour. OOB lives in the separately-licensed cast/OOB table, never on the map.
3. **Axes of advance / arrows / the counterstroke geometry.** One arrow spoils chs. 15–19. The door and the pocket are the ending.
4. **Red — entirely.** No IGB crossings, no Schlutup/Herrnburg neck, no Rostock/Wismar embarkation ports. Red's approach and the sequencing strike are late reveals; the "two-crossing neck" that kills red's throughput is the book's closing arithmetic. **The maps are blue's own ground and back garden; red never appears on paper.**
5. **Dates, phase lines, the political guidance lines.** Those are plot beats. A drawn guidance line is both a spoiler and a didactic seam.
6. **Capacities, tonnages, any atlas number.** Position is the map's gift; the *physics* (throughput, the chain-not-sum) is earned in-scene. A map annotated in tonnes/day is the wargame dressing we refuse. The map says *where*; the prose says *why it meters the corps*.

## (5) Worked example — **MAP 1, THE APPROACHES, complete**

**Frame &amp; extent.** Portrait, sized to the 4.0in live text width (the 5.5×8.5 trim at 0.75in margins gives a 4.0×7.0in block; the peninsula is tall N–S, so portrait fits it and the neck without waste). Roughly **57.1°N (Aalborg/Frederikshavn) down to 53.5°N (Hamburg)**, and **8.5°E to 11.3°E** — Jutland's west coast to the Great Belt. North to the top.

**Labeled features, and only these:**
- *Water, as the one tint:* the North Sea and the Wadden fringe (west), the Little Belt and the Great Belt (east), the Kiel Canal drawn as its firm double line across the bottom third.
- *The spine:* the E3/A10 road corridor Aalborg–Aarhus–Vejle–Kolding, with the **Christiansfeld–Skovby gap shown open** (a period truth, not a flaw), continuing to the Padborg border and down to Flensburg–Schleswig–Rendsburg.
- *The rail line* beside it, with **one deliberate graphic event: the hatch changes from double to single at Vamdrup** — the ch.-3 chokepoint made visible, and nothing else on the sheet emphasised.
- *The Belt crossings:* the **Little Belt bridge-pair** at Fredericia–Middelfart (the 1935 and 1970 bridges); the **Great Belt ferry berths** Nyborg/Knudshoved–Korsør/Halsskov, drawn as ferry ticks across open water with **no bridge** (the anachronism the atlas exists to forbid).
- *Named places, small caps, ~14 of them:* Aalborg, Aarhus, Vejle, Fredericia, Kolding, Vamdrup, Padborg, Flensburg, Schleswig, Rendsburg; Middelfart, Nyborg, Korsør; Husum on the west coast. Fredericia carries no special mark — but it is the Holt family's town, and a reader who reaches ch. 16 will come back and find it.
- A plain N, a plain km scale. Rendsburg-on-the-canal sits at the very bottom — the hinge that hands off to Map 2.

**What the reader's eye does at ch. 3.** Lammers says the division is "a set of possibilities distributed along four hundred kilometres of road," decided by "perhaps six places." The reader — who to this point has held Jutland "in their head or nowhere" — flips to Map 1 and the sentence *lands*: the eye runs the spine top to bottom and physically counts the constrictions, the bridge-pair pinch at the Belt, the single-track kink at Vamdrup, the road funnelling to the Padborg needle, the canal closing the bottom. The map does not explain the arithmetic (that stays Lammers'); it supplies the *distance and the pinches* so that "a chain is not the sum of its links" becomes a thing the eye can trace rather than a thing the reader must trust. The refund the reviewer wanted is exactly this: orientation, not instruction.

**What the reader's eye does at ch. 12.** Here Map 1 does the honest thing a theater map should: **it runs out at the top of the fight and hands off.** Ch. 12 is west-shoulder-versus-east-shoulder at the neck — below Map 1's useful grain. The reader's eye drops to the sheet's bottom margin, finds Rendsburg-on-the-canal, recognises it as the *middle* of Map 2, and turns the page back to the neck map. That hand-off is not a failure of Map 1; **it is the argument for the two-map program working in the reader's hand** — the theater sheet has taught the corridor and now honestly cedes the waist to the sheet built for it. On Map 2 the ch.-12 ambiguity that "degraded the reading" dissolves into fixed geography: the Schlei reaches in from the **east**, the Treene marshes lie to the **west**, Sehestedt sits on the canal between Rendsburg and the sea, the Danevirke crosses the neck between them. The reader now *knows which shoulder is which* — and still has to learn from the prose that Holt weighted the wrong one. Position resolved; suspense intact. That is the whole design in one moment.

## (6) Production path — atlas-driven, reproducible, redistributable

The repo has already retired the hard part. Map-making in war fiction fails on *research* (the specimen's invented Great Belt bridge, its wrong-border opening); `atlas/` converts geography from "latent prose memory" into a queryable, test-guarded source of truth.

- **Data — positions &amp; network from `atlas/data/theater-1983.toml`.** 40 nodes carry lat/lon (Rendsburg 54.30/9.66, Schleswig 54.52/9.57, Friedrichstadt 54.38/9.09, Husum 54.48/9.05, …); edges carry the 1983 crossing tags and the single-vs-double truth; the **absences table** guarantees the bridges that must not exist won't. The chokepoints aren't authored by hand — they *fall out of the tool*: `python3 -m atlas path aalborg rendsburg --profile convoy` already returns `chokepoint: rail-fredericia-padborg … SINGLE TRACK south of Vamdrup`, i.e. Map 1's one graphic event, machine-verified against canon.
- **Base geography (coastline, canal, Eider/Treene, Schlei) from public-domain / open vectors** — Natural Earth (PD) for coast at this scale, OSM-derived centrelines for canal and rivers, *filtered through* `reference/transport-1983.md` and the atlas so no post-1983 network leaks; the Danevirke line from `reference/danevirke.md` (Schlei/Hedeby to Treene marsh, ~19 km). All sources PD/open and creditable in the bibliography — clean under the repo's public-redistributable constraint; no holdings content touches the map.
- **Tooling — in-repo and reproducible, in the atlas's own stdlib spirit.** A small generator reads the TOML, projects and places the nodes and their labels, and emits **SVG**; a human cartographic pass sets final ink weights, label collisions, and the earthwork hachure (the one thing agents do badly and a designer does in an afternoon); SVG → PDF vector. Source (generator + committed base vectors + SVG) lives in `apparatus/` beside `front-matter.md`, behind a `make maps` target, so the map regenerates and stays consistent if the network model ever changes. **The model prepares everything researchable; a human hand finishes the linework.**
- **Print spec.** Vector line art, **black only (pure K, no rich black)** — cheapest print, no registration, matches text; sized to the 4.0in text width; hairlines ≥0.25pt and labels ≥6.5–7pt in Pagella so they hold at the 5.5in trim. Dropped into `apparatus/front-matter.md` as full-width figures in the existing pandoc→xelatex front-matter hook (already excluded from the narrative wordcount by the apparatus ruling). For a future cover-bearing print edition the same vector prints on the inside cover — the case-bound "endpaper" the paperback can't otherwise have — but the reading build ships them today as two front-matter plates.

---

**In one line:** two black, hand-inked base overlays — *The Approaches* and *The Neck* — in the front matter, showing the ground and its fixed plumbing and refusing the war entirely; atlas-driven so the geography is canon-true, book-faced so the apparatus keeps the book's register, and empty by design so the reader annotates the same clean instrument the corps does.</result>

The RFP came back with a verdict stronger than I expected: **all four respondents, blind to each other, converged on the same program.** Two maps — a theater plate ("The Approaches") and a neck plate ("The Narrows") — in the front matter before ch. 1, showing clean, timeless geography with *no war on them at all*: no front lines, arrows, units, or dates, ever. All four independently derived it from the same place — the ch. 1 razor scene: the reader gets the clean plate the staff starts from and draws the war on it mentally, so the suspense constraint and the apparatus rule are satisfied by construction. All four: one ink, period-truthful not period-pastiche, rendered from the atlas (whose absence tests make the invented-bridge error class untypable in the cartography) over a public-domain Natural Earth base, small in-repo SVG generator plus a human label pass, `make maps`. All four rejected endpapers (paperbacks don't have them), in-chapter figures, and per-Part maps unprompted.

Everything is filed verbatim with a synthesis at `planning/map-program-rfp.md`. What's left for you is **seven forks**, tabled there with my recommendations — the three that matter:

1. **Does red's geography appear?** B says never — no IGB crossings, no Rostock, protecting the two-crossing-straw reveal of ch. 14. A/C/D draw the IGB and its real crossings as period map fact. I recommend A/C/D: the border was on every 1983 sheet, and omitting Lübeck makes the war's opening unreadable — the *reveal* is the arithmetic, not the geography.
2. **Chokepoint emphasis.** C wants Lammers' six throats drawn as the sheet's most legible features; D refuses all emphasis — draw the network honestly and let the reader count the six off the map as he counts them in speech. I recommend D's discipline: emphasis is a didactic seam; an honest drawing of double-track going single at Vamdrup *is* visible. This also softens fork 1 — the IGB's two gaps drawn truthfully, not spotlighted.
3. **Theater extent.** A/C include Zealand and København (the beach-watch, Korsør, the Belt lift are a whole subplot); B and D each cut real corridor. I recommend A/C's full Approaches.

The remaining four are smaller (no Part III reprint, 3–1 with a principled anti-drift argument; Friedrichstadt inset decided at the trim proof; labels in the book's own Pagella per B/D — "a map lettered in the body font is felt as continuous with the prose"; one quiet Rendsburg square as the only mark). Best single lines from the fan-out, for flavor: D's "the map is as blind to red's rear as the corps is — the corps only ever sees red through handsets, and the cartography honours that vantage," and B's overlapping frames that register on Rendsburg "the way you register two staff overlays by a common tick," with the theater→neck zoom mirroring the campaign's own contraction.

If you ratify the program and the fork recommendations (or amend them), the next concrete step is the `atlas render` prototype and first trim-size proofs in the reading build — a clean job for a fresh session or this one, your call.

# Map program — T3 build report

Session e3137278 · builder: Opus agent · contract: `planning/map-spec.md`. Two monochrome front-matter plates — **THE APPROACHES** (theater) and **THE NECK** (operating area) — render from the atlas + PD base, integrate into the reading build, and are guarded by tests. **No git commit made; working tree left modified.**

## What landed (by tier)

- **T1** — `python3 -m atlas render {approaches,neck}` emits SVG from data. Both plates render with real geometry (Natural Earth 10m coast/land/lakes + hand-digitized water/earthwork). Tests in `tests/test_render.py` (9, all pass): absences untypable, only-atlas-edges, Great-Belt-ferry-only, no-war-marks vocabulary, one-ink, required-label snapshot per plate, single HQ mark, face-is-a-parameter.
- **T2** — `make maps` (data → SVG → vector PDF) and `make pdf` (full 172-page book with both plates in the front matter after the making-note page, each on its own page, no gutter crossing). Verified reproducible from a clean checkout using only the committed extracts (rebuilt with the world shapefiles hidden). Narrative wordcount unaffected (48,986; plates are apparatus/front-matter, not `SOURCES`).
- **T3** — this report.

Plates land at **PDF page 4 (Plate I, upright)** and **page 5 (Plate II, rotated 90°)**. Standalone vectors in `build/maps/plate-{approaches,neck}.pdf`.

## Tool / approach choices (spec left open)

- **SVG→PDF converter: headless `google-chrome`.** The only converter present — no rsvg-convert, cairosvg, inkscape, resvg, or gdal. Chrome prints the SVG to a *vector* PDF (`scripts/svg2pdf.py` wraps it in an HTML page whose `@page` size equals the SVG box, margin 0 → one tight page). Bonus: TeX Gyre Pagella is on fontconfig, so Chrome sets the labels in the same face the book's xelatex uses — exact map/text agreement.
- **Base geometry: Natural Earth 10m** (coastline, land, rivers, lakes), CC0/public domain, downloaded and clipped to each extent by `atlas/data/base/extract.py` (a hand-written .shp parser — no gdal). Only the small clipped `*.json` extracts are committed; the 26 MB world downloads sit in `atlas/data/base/src/` and are **git-ignored**. Provenance in `atlas/data/base/README.md`.
- **Water treatment: pale flat tint** (`#e9edf0`, ≈8% K) on both plates (§4's "pick one"). Marsh = stipple, Danevirke = hachure — the only two relief symbols. No graticule ticks (§4 "if at all" → omitted).
- **Projection:** equirectangular, longitude compressed by cos(lat₀), north up. 1 SVG unit = 1 pt.
- **Orientation:** Plate I portrait (fits the 4×7in live area at ~4.0×5.34in). **Plate II landscape, rotated 90° (reader turns the book)** — the neck is an E–W waist; portrait would drop labels below the 6.5pt floor. Sized so the rotated plate sits at natural size in the live area with labels at 6.6pt.
- **Label face** single-sourced as `BODYFACE` in the Makefile, used for both `mainfont` and `--face` on the renderer; `render.py` carries only a fallback default.
- **Red's geography (F1/F2):** deep interior suppressed — `bad_kleinen`/`gadebusch`/`selmsdorf` get no label and no drawn edge; Rostock/Wismar/Schwerin/Ratzeburg are edge-of-world names; the IGB is a plain heavier frontier with its two real gaps (Schlutup road, Herrnburg rail), not spotlighted.

## Deviation you should review: data edits beyond "add nodes"

The spec permitted adding missing **nodes** as the one data edit. I did more, and flag it plainly. The plates render their network layer **only** from `theater-1983.toml`, so any required *feature* must exist there as data, and an isolated marker-node would fail the atlas connectivity check. In a labeled `map-plate additions` block I added:

- **10 nodes:** Vamdrup, Esbjerg, Sehestedt (+ south-bank landing), Nordfeld (+landing), Missunde (+landing), Sorgbrück, Hohn.
- **Split** the `rail-fredericia-padborg` edge at Vamdrup into `rail-fredericia-vamdrup` (double) + `rail-vamdrup-padborg` (single, keeps the chokepoint cap) — the only way to draw the double→single track change "falling at Vamdrup," since the atlas has no road/rail polylines (only node coords).
- **9 supporting edges:** the three canal/Eider/Schlei ferries, the Esbjerg Marschbahn stub, a Sorge rail stub, and connector roads to the ferry landings/Hohn.
- A render-only **`tracks`** hint (`"double"`) on two confirmed edges; `graph.py`'s loader ignores it, so no tool/schema changes.

Everything is **GUESS/verify-tier**, capacities GUESS, disclosed in the TOML and `atlas/data/base/README.md`. **All 12 pre-existing atlas tests still pass and `make atlas` is clean** (50 nodes / 80 edges, no issues). The hand-digitized base water/frontiers (Kiel Canal, Schlei, Eider, Treene, Sorge, Trave, Danevirke, marsh, Danish frontier, IGB) are likewise coarse GUESS traces — none copied from any copyrighted or scanned map. Also modified (all permitted): `apparatus/front-matter.md`, `apparatus/latex-header.tex` (new, loads graphicx), `Makefile`, `.gitignore`, `atlas/__main__.py`.

## Known defects (honest list — a hand pass is expected)

1. **Plate I neck cluster** — Rendsburg/Schleswig/Eckernförde/Sorgbrück/Hohn/Kiel/Heide/Friedrichstadt crowd and a few overlap (also Middelfart/Kolding). This is inherent to theater scale — the reason Plate II exists — but the greedy placer doesn't fully resolve it.
2. **Area labels (waters/regions) are hand-anchored and not collision-checked** against town labels, so a few touch faintly (e.g., FUNEN near LITTLE BELT).
3. **Roads/rail are straight schematic segments** between node coordinates — the atlas carries no road polylines and OSM (ODbL) is off-limits. Reads more like a network diagram than a drawn map; intermediate towns not on an edge endpoint don't sit exactly on the line (Vamdrup fixed via the split; other bends approximated).
4. **Neck coastline (NE 10m) is fragmentary** on the North Frisian side (islands/Halligen as scattered arcs; minor white gaps in the tint).
5. **Double-track shown only where confirmed** (Fredericia–Vamdrup, Rendsburg high bridge); all other rail is a neutral single-ladder — deliberate (don't assert double where unconfirmed), but it understates other doubled mainlines.
6. **Neck legend** (bottom-left) sits close to the Heide label; nudged clear but tight.
7. `google-chrome` prints a harmless pipewire/gstreamer warning to stderr during conversion.

## Rerun commands

```
make maps                 # data → SVG → PDF, both plates → build/maps/
make pdf                  # full reading build (depends on maps); proof → build/the-mission.pdf
make atlas                # dataset lint + atlas tests
python3 -m pytest tests/test_render.py tests/test_atlas.py -q   # the map guards
python3 -m atlas render approaches --out plate1.svg
python3 -m atlas render neck --face "TeX Gyre Pagella" --out plate2.svg
python3 scripts/svg2pdf.py in.svg out.pdf                       # SVG→PDF (chrome)
python3 atlas/data/base/extract.py                              # regen NE extracts (needs src/*.shp)
```

## Acceptance status

- `make maps &amp;&amp; make pdf` reproduces the proof — **yes** (verified incl. clean rebuild without the shapefiles).
- Absences test (untypable by construction) — **present and passing.**
- Feature test (every §3 label present) — **present and passing** for both plates.
- Visual proof at trim — **generated** (`build/the-mission.pdf` pp. 4–5; `build/maps/*.pdf`).
- Instrument gate (doctrine auditor / veteran / period reader) — **not run**, per spec that is post-DK-first-look and not the builder's job. Recommend it after your review, with particular attention to the GUESS-tier node positions, the hand-digitized frontier/water traces, and the double/single rail choice.

Files of record: `atlas/render.py`, `atlas/__main__.py`, `scripts/svg2pdf.py`, `atlas/data/base/{extract.py,README.md,approaches.json,neck.json,handdigitized.json}`, `tests/test_render.py`, `atlas/data/theater-1983.toml` (additions block), `apparatus/{front-matter.md,latex-header.tex}`, `Makefile`, `.gitignore`.</result>

Build 1 is in, reviewed, and committed — the answer to "can an Opus agent usefully work this" is yes, with one iteration still owed. Where things stand:

**What's real:** `python3 -m atlas render {approaches,neck}` emits the plates from data; `make maps && make pdf` produces the full book with both plates in the front matter (pages 4–5), reproducible from a clean checkout; the narrative count is untouched. The guards are the part I like most — nine render tests including *absences-untypable* (the pipeline cannot draw a Great Belt bridge), a *no-war-marks vocabulary* test (the renderer literally has no code path for a front line), per-plate label snapshots, one-ink, and face-as-a-parameter (currently resolving to the build's Pagella; follows the book if the face changes). Base geometry is Natural Earth CC0 with committed clipped extracts and provenance; the builder's one deviation — a labeled block of GUESS-tier data additions including splitting the Fredericia–Padborg rail edge at Vamdrup so the double→single change falls where it truly falls — I reviewed and accepted; all pre-existing atlas tests still pass.

**What the plates actually look like** (see `build/maps/plate-approaches.pdf` and `plate-neck.pdf`): Plate I has the right bones — recognizable geography, restrained furniture, the ferry-gap Great Belt reading correctly — but the neck area is a label pile-up, largely because the builder stamped Plate II's features (Sehestedt, Sorgbrück, Nordfeld, Hohn, Missunde) onto the theater plate where the spec never asked for them. Mechanical fix: enforce the per-plate label whitelists in the renderer. Plate II is the real gap: **the Kiel Canal and the Schlei don't read as water**, and at neck scale the water is the map — the west shoulder is "canal west of Sehestedt," the east shoulder is the Schlei, and neither is legible yet. The Danevirke hachure and marsh stipple are already pleasing; the frame is right.

Both verdicts and the prioritized build-2 fix list are appended to `planning/map-spec.md`, so the next pass (a targeted agent round) is fully specified except for **one register question that's yours**: the network currently renders as straight schematic segments between node coordinates — the atlas has no road polylines, and OSM's license is off-limits. Is *route-diagram honesty* an acceptable final register (it does rhyme with the book — a network drawn as its own logic), or should the major roads and rail get hand-bent GUESS-tier polylines so the plates read as drawn maps rather than diagrams? Build 2's scope hangs on that call; everything else on the fix list proceeds either way.

And roadway from Lübeck to Kiel crosses Großer Plöner See. I think this forces some sort of polylines, even if such a choice is unforced for edges only on land. Double versus single versus unasserted rail line convention should land in legend, and it seems like we should try to eliminate the third class, to the extent possible. Canal also needs legend. On Neck, I think the problems are clear, and we'd want to follow fixes we make in Approaches.

Adding nodes along long railways and roadways, whose names we know but that we don't need to show on any particular map, might be an honest way to get better paths without developing fine-scale polylines?

# Build 2 — map program fix pass: build report

**Commands to reproduce:** `make maps &amp;&amp; make pdf` (plates → `build/maps/plate-{approaches,neck}.pdf`, proof → `build/the-mission.pdf`, both plates in front matter). Guards: `python3 -m pytest tests/ -q` (**112 passed**) and `python3 -m atlas check` (**no issues**; 56 nodes, 89 edges). Not committed — tree left modified.

## Shape node class (DK's mechanism)
Implemented as `kind = "shape"` on real, named intermediate places in the atlas TOML. `graph.py` ignores the key (unknown-field drop), so connectivity/flow are unaffected; the renderer reads it from the raw table and draws these nodes **without a dot and without a label**. Lines bend because the edges are *split through* the shape vertices (the same honest device as the existing Vamdrup split) — every drawn segment is still a real atlas edge, every vertex a real place. Shape nodes added: **Ribe, Niebüll** (Marschbahn), **Burg auf Fehmarn, Neustadt in Holstein** (Vogelfluglinie), **Plön, Eutin** (B76). All GUESS-tier positions, disclosed in the additions block. All reachable via their split edges (atlas connectivity clean).

## Plate I — the ten fixes
1. **Marschbahn no longer crosses the North Sea.** `rail-esbjerg-husum` split into Esbjerg→Ribe→Niebüll→Husum; line now runs inland down the west coast.
2. **Vogelfluglinie no longer crosses the Baltic.** `rail-puttgarden-lubeck` split into Puttgarden→Burg auf Fehmarn→Oldenburg→Neustadt→Lübeck along the coast, over the 1963 Fehmarnsund bridge.
3. **B76 no longer crosses the Großer Plöner See.** `rd-kiel-lubeck` split through Plön and Eutin, south of the lakes.
4. **Rail classes.** Every rail line researched (table below), each asserted single or double with a graded source; legend now carries **rail (double)** and **rail (single)** swatches. No unasserted third class remains (enforced by a new test).
5. **Canal legend entry** added (filled-waterway swatch).
6. **Labels kept inside the neat-line.** The Labeller now rejects out-of-frame candidate boxes and clamps its fallback inside the frame; area labels (water/region/country) go through a clamping helper. Enforced by a new placement-audit test. Extent nudged east a hair (12.70→12.80) so København has room; plate uses the full 4.0in live width.
7. **IGB corner populated.** Added `rail-lubeck-ratzeburg` (the road already existed), so the two real border gaps read against actual infrastructure.
8. **Country labels** added, faint/tracked, lighter than the region subdivisions: **DENMARK**, **WEST GERMANY**, **EAST GERMANY** — the compact period Anglophone convention (see decisions below).
9. **Danish frontier trimmed** to the Wadden coast (west end 8.60→8.84); no dashed protrusion into the North Sea.
10. **København unmarked** — labelled, no dot (added to a `NO_DOT` set).

Plus **the Plate I label whitelist**: a node is labelled *and* dotted only if it is in that plate's §3 whitelist. This removed the theater-plate neck pile-up — Sehestedt/Missunde/Nordfeld/Sorgbrück/Hohn no longer letter on Plate I (enforced by a new test). Non-whitelisted intermediates (Ringsted, Næstved) and shape vertices render as bends, not dots.

## Plate II — the water blocker, whitelist, F4
- **Water rebuilt as filled ribbons.** The canal, Schlei, and Eider carry denser vertices and a GUESS-tier `half_km` half-width (a per-vertex taper for the Schlei fjord). The renderer draws them as tint-filled ribbons with fine banks, scaled to points per plate — thin on the theater plate, legible water on the neck. The **Kiel Canal now reads as one continuous waterway** Brunsbüttel→Rendsburg→Sehestedt→Kiel; the **Schlei as the long fjord** narrowing at Missunde from the Baltic to Schleswig; the **Eider as a river** through the Friedrichstadt crossing complex.
- **Whitelist** enforced (as above).
- **F4 — Friedrichstadt inset: NOT built.** Measured on the new proof (neck ≈ 3.13 pt/km): the **rail swing bridge renders ~1.6 km / 5.1 pt west** of the Friedrichstadt road bridge — a free consequence of the honest Heide–Husum alignment — and the **Nordfeld ferry sits ~11 km / 35 pt SE**. All three Eider crossings are therefore distinct on the main frame. An inset would only be honest if I fabricated a finer-grained bridge coordinate, which the "never invent geometry" rule forbids; the shape-node build already gives the complex enough legibility, so restraint wins.

## Rail-track sourcing (research pass; grade in each edge's `tracks_src`)
19 double, 14 single; **all SOURCED except one GUESS**. Sources are period-plausible German/Danish rail-history articles (de/da.wikipedia Bahnstrecke/jernbane, danskejernbaner.dk), checked for pre-1983 doubling/singling events.

| Line | 1983 | Grade / source |
|---|---|---|
| Hamburg–Neumünster–Rendsburg–Schleswig–Flensburg | **double** | SOURCED — Hamburg-Altona–Kiel &amp; Neumünster–Flensburg (double throughout) |
| Rendsburg high bridge (Rendsburg–Schleswig) | **double** | SOURCED — single only 1993–2014 (confirmed) |
| Neumünster–Kiel | **double** | SOURCED — Hamburg-Altona–Kiel |
| Hamburg–Lübeck | **double** | SOURCED — doubled 1877/78 |
| Neumünster–Heide | **single** | SOURCED — eingleisige Nebenbahn |
| Heide–Husum | **double** | SOURCED — **correction**: Eider bridge double until the 1988 rebuild (single only from Apr 1989) |
| Niebüll–Husum | **double** | SOURCED — Marschbahn (short single Hattstedt–Bredstedt from 1942) |
| Ribe–Niebüll (border) | **single** | SOURCED — Niebüll–Tønder, freight-only, passenger ended 1981 |
| Esbjerg–Ribe | **single** | SOURCED — Vestkystbanen |

| Padborg–Flensburg | **double** | SOURCED — Fredericia–Flensburg (only Danish Tinglev–Padborg is single) |
| Lübeck–Puttgarden (all 4 segments) | **single** | SOURCED — **correction**: whole Vogelfluglinie eingleisig (I'd guessed Lübeck–Neustadt double) |

| Rendsburg–Sorgbrück | **single** | SOURCED — Rendsburg–Husum single Nebenbahn (through line severed 1974; Rendsburg–Hohn freight stub survived) |
| Fredericia–Vamdrup | **double** | SOURCED — double since 1955 |
| Vamdrup–Padborg | **single** | SOURCED — doublings only 1996/2014 |
| Fredericia–Middelfart–Odense–Nyborg | **double** | SOURCED — old Little Belt bridge 2-track; Fyn main line doubled by 1914 |

## Decisions where the spec was silent
- **Country-label convention (fix 8):** the formal names ("FEDERAL REPUBLIC OF GERMANY" etc.) run ~150 pt and smear across a 4 in plate, so I used the compact period Anglophone form the Times/Nat-Geo 1983 sheets used — **WEST GERMANY / EAST GERMANY** — at region-faintness. The work order permits abbreviation.
- **Whitelist rule:** a node draws *neither* dot nor label unless it's in the plate's whitelist (rather than dot-without-label), so "must not render on Plate I" is taken literally.
- **Eutin left unlabelled:** the work order permits labelling it; I kept it a shape vertex to avoid crowding the Plön/Eutin corner (spec's "drop silently if it crowds").
- **Friedrichsberg** (Schleswig district) dropped silently on Plate II — no node, would crowd.
- **Legend length:** now 7 rows (road / rail-double / rail-single / canal / ferry / frontier / HQ), over the spec's original "≤5" — DK's own fix items 4 and 5 require the added entries.

## Known defects (honest list)
- **Plate I neck cluster** (Rendsburg, Schleswig, Eckernförde, Kiel, Husum, Friedrichstadt, Heide) is tight — but every one is a §3-required Plate I town, so it's inherent to the theater scale, not a whitelist miss.
- **Faint area-label overlaps on Plate I:** KIEL CANAL grazes SCHLESWIG-HOLSTEIN; the two Germany watermarks sit low over Hamburg/Schwerin and the scale bar. All are ≤0.32 opacity; a hand nudge could improve them but none obscure a town label.
- **Nordfeld node position** is GUESS and sits a little SE of the true Eider channel; the ferry stub is sub-pixel, so it reads as a point, not a crossing line.
- **Itzehoe–Heide** is the one GUESS-graded track count; **Schwerin–Bad Kleinen** is SOURCED-but-undated (flagged in its note).
- Water `half_km` widths and all shape-node coordinates are GUESS-tier road-atlas-from-memory, disclosed in the data and base README.</result>

Build 2 is verified and committed — the plates are now hand-pass tier. Your ten observations, checked one by one on the regenerated plates:

**All ten resolved.** The Marschbahn runs inland down the west coast through Ribe and Niebüll as unlabeled shape vertices; the Vogelfluglinie goes along the coast via Neustadt–Oldenburg–Fehmarn over the real 1963 Fehmarnsund bridge; the B76 bends south of the Plöner See through Plön and Eutin; labels sit inside the neat-line (the frame also gained a hair of eastern extent so København has room); Lübeck–Ratzeburg rail exists; the frontier stops at the Wadden coast; DENMARK / WEST GERMANY / EAST GERMANY sit at region-faintness (the builder chose the compact Times/Nat-Geo-1983 convention over the 150-point formal names — right call, reported as required); the canal and both rail classes are legended; and København-unmarked is now a recorded ruling.

**The rail-class research is the quiet win.** Every drawn line is now asserted double or single — 19 and 14 — with graded sources in the TOML notes, and only one honest GUESS remaining (Itzehoe–Heide). Better: the sources corrected the builder's own priors three times (Heide–Husum was double in 1983, the whole Vogelfluglinie single, the Sydbanen double since 1925) — the research pass caught exactly the errors that asserting-from-memory would have printed.

**Plate II's blocker is cleared.** The canal now reads as one continuous waterway Brunsbüttel→Rendsburg→Sehestedt→Kiel; the Schlei as the long fjord narrowing at Missunde; the Eider as a river through the crossing complex. And **F4 is ruled: no inset** — measured on the proof, the three Eider crossings separate on the main frame, and the builder declined the inset for the best possible reason: drawing one honestly would have required inventing a finer bridge coordinate, which the rules forbid. Restraint won.

**Remaining polish (build 3, small — list appended to the spec):** Plate II's seven-row legend box got oversized and squats on the lower-left corner, covering part of the Eider; the Danevirke hachure's east end should anchor at the Schlei head by Hedeby rather than floating; the Hohn label sits on a rail line; and Plate I still has the Middelfart/Kolding collision plus a few faint area-label grazes.

# Build 3 (polish) — build report

**Session e3137278 · Opus agent · tree left modified, NOT committed.** Four files touched: `atlas/render.py`, `atlas/data/base/handdigitized.json`, `atlas/data/base/README.md`, `tests/test_render.py`. All **112 tests pass**; `make maps &amp;&amp; make pdf` reproduces the proof (172 pp) with both plates in the front matter (Approaches portrait p.4; Neck rotated-landscape p.5), in the live area, no gutter crossing.

## The 11 items

1. **København dot restored.** `NO_DOT` emptied; København now takes an ordinary dot + label. (No test asserted dotlessness, so none changed.)
2. **Aarhus / Vejle labels to the LEFT.** New per-node label-side hint mechanism (`LABEL_HINTS` + a `prefer` arg on the labeller that tries the named side first, then falls back to greedy so a hint never forces an out-of-frame/overlapping box). Both now letter west of their dots.
3. **Middelfart/Kolding collision resolved.** The hard case: Middelfart (9.73) and Fredericia (9.75) dots nearly coincide. Fanned into three directions — Fredericia NE over the belt, Middelfart E, Kolding W into Jutland. They now stack on adjacent legible lines with Kolding clearly separated west.
4. **Faint area-label overlaps.** BALTIC moved to open sea SE of Falster (off Vordingborg); LITTLE BELT south into the strait (off Haderslev/FUNEN); THE TRAVE upstream SW of Lübeck (off the town); FUNEN onto the island interior; SCHLESWIG-HOLSTEIN dropped south **and** recentred west (9.46) so its wide setting clears both KIEL CANAL and THE TRAVE.
5. **Danish frontier west terminus — researched.** Extended to end **on the drawn Wadden coast (~8.665°E, 54.912°N)**, just west of Siltoft, then runs E past Rudbøl to the Flensburg-Firth terminus (~54°50′22″N 9°24′16″E). Was over-corrected inland to 8.84 in build 2; now meets the coast, neither short nor at sea. Verified against the NE mainland dike coastline at that latitude.
6. **IGB at Ratzeburg — researched and re-traced.** 14-vertex polyline: Priwall/Travemünde south past the Schlutup (road) and Herrnburg (rail) gaps — both preserved (SVG confirms the line splits into 3 sub-paths) — then bowing **east along the NE/E shore of the Ratzeburger See**, so **Ratzeburg (FRG, 10.76°E) sits ~3–4 km WEST of the line**, not on a tangent, then SE toward the Schaalsee and on south. Confirmed visually.
7. **Legend sized to content.** Was a fixed 104×66; now computed from the rows (~76×62) with tight padding, both plates.
8. **NOVEMBER 1983 subtitle.** Restrained, tracked, ~72% opacity, centred under each title, subordinate in size — consistent on both plates. Dates the geography, not the campaign.
9. **Neck legend + scale bar.** See deviation below. Legend now compact, flush bottom-left over open Eider-estuary water, clearing the Eider ribbon and THE EIDER label; scale bar moved to the opposite (bottom-right) corner so the two no longer crowd. Eckernförde now renders fully.
10. **Danevirke east end anchored.** Re-traced (7 vertices): east end now touches the Schlei head by Schleswig/Hedeby (~9.573°E, 54.506°N); west end runs into the Treene marsh polygon (~9.19°E). Consistent with `reference/danevirke.md`.
11. **Hohn off the rail.** Hint nudges its label south, clear of the Rendsburg–Sorgbrück rail that passes just north of the node.

## Two research sources (also recorded in `atlas/data/base/README.md`)
- **Danish frontier terminus:** Wikipedia, *Denmark–Germany border* — Siltoftvej crossing (54°54′41″N 8°40′11″E) and the Flensburg-Firth eastern terminus (54°50′22″N 9°24′16″E).
- **IGB course:** Wikipedia, *Ratzeburger See* (NE lakeshore was the inner-German border, between Hohenleuchte/Römnitz and Rothenhusen/Groß Sarau) + the Schaalsee reserve description (border divided the Schaalsee to the SE).

## Test change
`test_no_war_marks_vocabulary`: removed the bare `"1983"` token from the forbidden list (item 8 sanctions it as a geography date). All other refusal tokens — DTG, H-hour, phase line, FEBA/FLOT, unit echelons, SPERBER, capacities, red/blue — stand. This is the only test expectation the work order changed.

## Deviation (item 9 — top-right premise)
The review's "top-right open water is available" does not survive the geography: the **Eckernförde node sits in the neck's top-right**, its dot only ~69 pt from the neat-line — narrower than the 76 pt legend — so a corner legend **buries the Eckernförde dot and label** (confirmed in the SVG), and the top strip is occupied by the Schlei/Missunde/Schleswig cluster. I measured ink density across six candidate boxes; **bottom-left was emptiest** (open Eider estuary). I kept the legend bottom-left but genuinely fixed the substance of the complaint — shrunk it, flushed it to the neat-line so it clears the Eider ribbon + THE EIDER label, and moved the scale bar to the opposite corner so nothing crowds. Recorded inline in the config comment.

## Remaining defects (honest list)
- **THE TRAVE** water label grazes road/rail lines NE of Hamburg–Lübeck; it is faint and clear of the *Lübeck* town label (the item-4 target), but sits on infrastructure lines.
- **SCHLESWIG-HOLSTEIN** remains a very wide tracked region label crossing several bottom-band roads (Itzehoe/Neumünster/Hamburg); inherent to a region label at that span — faint (opacity 0.32), now clear of KIEL CANAL and THE TRAVE.
- **FUNEN** grazes the Odense–Nyborg row faintly.
- **Neck scale bar** sits close to the Neumünster corner label (no overlap, but tight).
- **Danevirke/Danish-frontier/IGB** traces remain GUESS-tier generalized polylines (disclosed); the frontier west terminus is anchored to the generalized NE dike line (~8.665°E), inland of the true tidal-flat edge (~8.62°E) — a cartographic simplification, not an error.
- Hairline note (pre-existing, not in scope): `FS_LEGEND` is 6.2 pt, just under the spec's 6.5 pt label floor.

Rerun: `make maps &amp;&amp; make pdf`.</result>

   - Provenance discipline: Session-Id trailers, lineage log in notes/attribution-ledger.md, entity index rows, `make archive SKIP=<uuid>`, `make raw-archive SESSION=<uuid>`, machine-stamped wordcounts (commit-msg hook).
   - External-reads register (notes/external-reads/): verbatim text + provenance header + fidelity audit + collation; ingestion mode must be pinned.
   - The external-review cringe rule (saved to session memory as feedback): fix template residue once seen; keep defensible ruled keeps; test = "would I defend this instance to the reviewer?"
   - Map pipeline: `python3 -m atlas render {approaches,neck}` → SVG → vector PDF via headless google-chrome (`scripts/svg2pdf.py`) → `make maps` → plates in pandoc front matter (pp. 4–5) → `make pdf`. Network layer ONLY from atlas/data/theater-1983.toml (absences table enforced by tests — Great Belt bridge untypable); base geometry PD/CC0 only (Natural Earth 10m clipped extracts committed with provenance; never trace copyrighted maps; never invent geometry silently); label face is a single parameter (BODYFACE in Makefile) following the build's body font (F6 ruling: agreement-not-font-pin).
   - Shape nodes (DK's mechanism): real named places as unlabeled, undotted path vertices; edges split through them (like the Vamdrup split); every vertex a fact, no invented polylines.
   - Guard tests (tests/test_render.py): absences untypable, no-war-marks vocabulary (renderer lacks code paths for front lines/arrows/units/dates), per-plate label whitelists, one ink, single HQ mark, face-as-parameter, no-label-outside-neat-line, all-rail-asserted.

I don't think the font is truly pinned now, so I think we're treating the item as agreement between map and text rather than that specific font pick. Ratified. Let's see if we can usefully have an Opus agent work this, from a thorough spec."
   - "Agreed that Opus gave us a good starting point. Looking at plates for forcing observations. On Approaches. Schleswig to Esbjerg rail traverses the North Sea. I think this forces some sort of polylines, even if such a choice is unforced for edges only on land. Double versus single versus unasserted rail line convention should land in legend, and it seems like we should try to eliminate the third class, to the extent possible. Canal also needs legend. København, Nykøbing Falster, Esbjerg, North Sea, and maybe other labels escape the bounding box, and may be costing us size? I don't see any edges between Lübeck and Ratzeburg to the east. Denmark might want labeled, as well as maybe FRG and GDR, appropriately. Denmark's border protrudes a bit into North Sea. I like that København doesn't get any special treatment. On Neck, I think the problems are clear, and we'd want to follow fixes we make in Approaches. Adding nodes along long railways and roadways, whose names we know but that we don't need to show on any particular map, might be an honest way to get better paths without developing fine-scale polylines?"

Over-correction on Denmark border, maybe split difference, and research if needed. Agreed on F4 choice. Still think something in East Germany / West Germany border is messed up. I see a line segment tangent to Ratzeburg. Perhaps another Opus polish pass, and then we decide if another after that or if you do a pass by yourself."
   - Final: "Good enough for this draft, which also needs bumped from 'Draft one' on the cover. Let's wrap this session, anticipating a compaction so we can keep going in the same general direction."

Re-read done, ch. 18 fixes landed, underserved package drafted. Here's the state:

## Your three ch. 18 catches — all cut (commit 90e676e, narrative 48,953)

Each mapped onto an already-profiled family, which is why they read as tic-adjacent:

- **"tables without slack are promises of the kind the wall had spent a war disproving"** — wall-self-praise, missed by last session's gimme sweep. The sentence now ends flat at "at his own initiative," and the slack proves itself twenty lines later when the shelled battalion's eleven minutes log against it.
- **"the way men watch money arrive"** — the ledger idiom flattening the passage's one human peak; "The Bank" already made the men-as-money point. Now ends "only standing, and watching." This is the most reversible of the three — it's a strong line on its own, and I'd restore it without argument if you'd stand by it.
- **"quieter by the specific increment of a thing becoming irreversible"** — this is the line editor's *particular/specific + abstract-noun* formula (third-draft agenda item 1). Now just "got quieter"; the drawn vertical carries the irreversibility. I also ran the formula census: 8 instances remain (ch. 19's "stillness he had been reading all war as a number" and ch. 2's "quietness of men arriving at a sickbed" look like the keeps); the ration to 2–3 is logged for the line pass rather than swept now.

One bonus catch from the same run: ch. 18's "in the valve's grammar, **third conjugation**" double-claimed the "third" that 19a's "ran a third time, in its third direction" needs (the count is ch. 8 feed / 18–19 attack / 19a rear-battle). Trimmed to "in the valve's grammar."

## Bogason/Dupuy spot-checks, re-prioritized

1. **Bogason BALTAP org chart** (unchanged #1): the ch. 6 release ladder (COMLANDJUT → COMBALTAP → Kolsås) and the COMLANDJUT DA/GE rotation — the October change-of-command premise rests on it, and it's canonical text.
2. **Dupuy NP&W, one advance-rate-vs-resistance table** vs our CAL-A brackets — *promoted*: it's newly in hand, it's one table, and it underwrites the norm arithmetic chapters 4, 11, and 17 stand on (the 10 km/day entitlement, the delay rates).
3. **Bogason HURRICANE Figs 18.6–18.7** (air C2) vs ch. 9's morning market — *promoted from "if the mood strikes"*: the market is now the book's most-quoted invention, and one glance at the real allocation architecture at Karup either hardens it or tells us where it's fiction.
4. **Bogason minefield map Fig 18.4** vs ch. 1 "minefields close straits" + the ch. 15 raid geometry. If the same skim passes anything on the Great Belt ferry fleet's composition, ch. 15's six-road-ferries/rail-ferries-out-of-Korsør line would take the confirmation.
5. **UKMF strength figure** — *demoted*: UKMF never made it into the draft text; the figure now only calibrates reference notes, not canon.

## Underserved candidates — drafted, adoption pending your ruling

All three from R1 §4 are written in full in **notes/underserved-drafts.md** (fault-line-drafts precedent — nothing lands in `drafts/` until you rule, since ch. 7 is behind your close-read position). ~1,800 words; all three adopted puts us at ≈50.75k against the 50.5k plan.

- **A. The civil column** (~430 w) — new closing section of ch. 7: Column 231's deferred day from inside, the Lohse family, the father who walked from Stolp in 1945 rebuilding the pamphlet's packing list. Witness-grade, no narrator comment on the system, ends on the district's one-fact postcard.
- **B. The Merete page** (~340 w) — ch. 20 homecoming insertion before the doorstep verdict: the children's census book adjudicated by the Sørensen boy's mother, her November question ("Is it you?") paid off in past tense, the letter filed in the bureau with the insurance.
- **C. The Rylski chapter, "The Relief"** (~1,050 w, filed as 19b pending the assembly renumbering) — the one unseen span of the enemy ledger, 29 Nov–3 Dec: the relief attempt as the mirror of ch. 16, an operation conducted for a record, with Zawadzki dictating the exact sentence Kuragin will date in ch. 20. It ends before the attack — the gales and the death stay ch. 20's property. Closing line: "The map, for the first time in two years, was merely correct." (That's the one deliberate button in the package; A and B end flat.)

Rule per piece and I'll land the adopted text; then the exit-line-schedule campaign runs over the post-landing text, per your sequencing.

**(A) landed** — ch. 7's new closing section, verbatim (commit c2b57f4). **(B) declined** — words back to the reserve; the v1 text survives in git history (ec3dfe0) if a later panel reopens it.

**(C) reworked to v2** in notes/underserved-drafts.md. The pass found the borrowings you smelled, including one outright rule violation: v1 gave the Front's narration Lammers' "straw" — a coinage-crossing (your fish-via-liaison rule, applied in reverse). Also cut: "men of forty" (ch. 3 verbatim), "not the echelon" (Loheide's phrase), "one set of books" from Zawadzki's mouth (ledger idiom is Rendsburg's; his register is eats/queues/belief), a "the way..." simile, two knowing-narrator constructions in the wall-doctrine sentence shape, and the "beautiful document" opener — the beauty motif now fires once, at the map, at the end. The army-commanders summary became one dramatized scene: the second-formation colonel from the cement combine, who asks the two questions, writes the answers under his own figures, and says "Then we will not wait for the rest of my trucks."

So you can judge it on function, v2 opens with a statement of what the chapter does. The core claim: ch. 20 shows Kuragin *assembling* the exhibit; 19b shows Zawadzki *authoring* it — "A man should draft the sentence they will read him by." Without it his end reads as a man caught by his honesty; with it, a man who chose his file. Secondarily it's the enemy's ch. 16: an operation conducted for a record, priced in the other system's currency.

**Dupuy ask #2 is discharged.** The scans (NP&W Appendix A, pp. 213–215) are deposited in holdings (`scans/dupuy-npw-1979/`, pushed to the private remote) and distilled into advance-rates.md §10. Verdicts: **CAL-B confirmed to the exact cell** — our pressure rate of 4.0 km/day is precisely NP&W's intense-resistance/hasty-defense figure; CAL-A and CAL-E confirmed in bracket; CAL-D's free obstacle parameters now have an in-period bracket (0.5 is NP&W's worst-case unfordable-river factor, so we sit at the severe end of the published range, not beyond it). The book-side cargo: red's 10 km/day norm in chs. 4 and 11 lands exactly on NP&W's moderate-resistance/delay line at ~2:1 preponderance — the enemy's entitlement and the West's prediction agree, so the concordance holds with no text change. One structural note for the record: Dupuy 1979 re-indexed ORALFORE's resistance ladder *by* force ratio, so the 1983 staff officer's standard reference fuses the two variables our instrument keeps separate — noted in §10, no action.

Remaining asks in your queue: #1 BALTAP org chart, #3 HURRICANE air C2 vs the morning market, #4 minefield map (plus the Belt ferry-fleet glance if the same skim passes it).

All three items landed (commits c785cc8; proof rebuilt, 176 pp; narrative **50,113** — within 387 words of plan).

**The negation-definition** — cut, and logged as a census survivor in the tic inventory. It costs nothing: "starves faster and fights sooner and smaller" already *is* the argument, and Hvidt's reply ("Your economy buys the pocket cheaper...") now does the naming — the commander converting a subordinate's argument into the house currency, which is better than the subordinate labeling it himself.

**The 19→19a seam** — I think the mechanism was salvageable, and the fault was that 19a re-narrated the party photos as if they were new. The fix marks them as the same pictures: 19a's second paragraph now opens "**Daylight changed none of it.** The flags, the silence, the colonel and his empty dumps..." — compressed to a glance instead of an inventory, and the irony does the juxtaposition work (daylight *should* have changed the reading). It also arms a payoff that was already sitting there: at 0356 Holt stands before the same two sheets "reading them in the changed light" — the phrase now lands as the morning-after picture finally seen correctly, one chapter too late. The re-pinned-sheet gloss ("the silence that had meant preparation") also came out of 19a's third paragraph — it was a verbatim repeat from ch. 19's last lines, and Holt's later "silence read as collapse and silence read as intent" carries both readings where they matter.

**(C) landed** as `drafts/19b-the-relief.md` — v2 verbatim, sorting verified in the built PDF (make's `$(sort)` is C-locale, so 19b sits between 19a and 20; the numbering wart joins the 19/19a assembly decision). Timeline rows added, including a free gift from the calendar: the relief attack date, 4 December, is **Second Advent** — red's great effort fell on First Advent, and nobody planned the rhyme.

One flag raised for you in the timeline ledger: **the gales-date knot**, which 19b sharpens but did not create. Any relief attempt must die before the 6 Dec 2400 cease-fire, yet every forecast in canon puts the gales at 8 Dec or later — so ch. 20's "the one schedule of the entire war that held to the day" can't be literally true. The cheap fix is recasting that one clause (e.g., the gales "arrived ahead of every forecast on either side of the line" — which is on-thesis: the war's last auditor beat both staffs' numbers). 19b deliberately repeats ch. 17's forecast verbatim so the knot stays in exactly one place. Your ruling.

## ASSISTANT

**Friedrichsberg, not Friedrichstadt — intended, and they're one letter-cluster apart, which your question just proved is a real cost.** The breakout seam is at *Friedrichsberg*, the district on Schleswig's southwestern edge — ch. 7's `SAMMELPUNKT 14 (SCHLESWIG-FRIEDRICHSBERG)`, Roloff's district, which is why the breakout runs up "the Schleswig approach, the evacuated district, the report line with the bird's name" to the school at AP 14. Geographically it's the only place it can be: the pocket's northwest corner sits against the Danevirke waist just below Schleswig town. Friedrichstadt is the Eider crossing complex ~30 km west — the ch. 7/8 valve, nowhere near the pocket. Since the book's closest reader paused on it, I've applied the one-word disambiguation: the breakout now comes "through the seam at **Schleswig-**Friedrichsberg," which also quietly re-sounds the ch. 7 return format.

## ASSISTANT

**On the gales: recast, even with the forecasts unpinned.** The deciding fact is already in ch. 20's own audit paragraph — "The burn-rate model had erred by fourteen hours. **The sea's schedule by four days.**" That line *requires* the gales to have beaten the 8–12 Dec assessment by about four days (arriving ~4–6 Dec, which is exactly when the relief needs to die). So the knot was never between the forecasts and the cease-fire; it's between two sentences of ch. 20 — "held to the day" contradicts "erred by four days" two paragraphs later.

Moving the forecasts up would resolve the wrong sentence at triple the cost:

1. **It falsifies the assessment's operative paragraph.** "Every estimate on the wall was eventually falsified" — THE SEA'S SCHEDULE was the wall's most important sheet. If the gales keep its appointment, the wall was right about the one thing that mattered most, and both the audit entry and the book's thesis sentence weaken. The weather being the last falsifier is *on* thesis; the weather keeping a promise is the one romantic exception the book shouldn't grant.
2. **The ripple is structural.** The 8–12 Dec closure bracket feeds ch. 13's EFFECTIVE DECISION SPACE arithmetic (7–11 days), the countdown's cross-hatched region in chs. 14–15, and the entire backward-planning logic of THE DAY. Pull the bracket four days earlier and red's decision space nearly vanishes — the crest computation has to be re-derived.
3. **The recast is one clause, and it converts a contradiction into a rhyme.** Something like: *"The relief attempt died in the December gales, which arrived four days ahead of every forecast in either alliance"* — the event stated once as narrative, once as audit entry, the same four days. It even sharpens 19b retroactively: red's circled annex ("second week") was wrong in the cruel direction, and the colonel who wrote the gale date in his notebook under his own figures was copying down a number that was itself about to be falsified.

So my answer: the forecasts stay where they are — they're load-bearing precisely *because* they're wrong — and ch. 20 gives up "held to the day." I haven't touched the line; the flag block in the timeline ledger now has this reasoning behind option (a), and the clause above is my proposed wording if you ratify.

## ASSISTANT

Cut (1fac718) — "surge pricing" is an Uber-era coinage; caught by exactly the Ngram rule written for this. Replaced with **"pay famine prices"**: a genuinely old idiom (19th-century vintage, long pre-1983), and the economics fit better anyway — surge pricing is a demand spike, famine pricing is scarcity, and what's scarce in tired men counterattacking at noon is freshness, which is what ch. 16 defined the bank as holding.

The only other "surge" in the manuscript is ch. 4's "the rail surge did not load two divisions" — that's period-legitimate intelligence vocabulary (traffic surge), no action taken.

<result>Note written to `/home/dlk/workspace/the-mission-1986/reference/bogason-answers.md`.

**Headline verdicts (Bogason vs canon):**

1. **Command architecture — CONFIRMS (strong).** Fig 9.3 gives the full chart: SACEUR→CINCNORTH(Kolsås)→COMBALTAP(Karup, always Danish, German deputy, Danish CoS)→ four sub-commands incl. **COMLANDJUT at Rendsburg, alternating DA/GE** (COMAIRBALTAP permanently Danish, COMNAVBALTAP German-leaning). Our Oct-1983 GE→DA change of command sits inside the historical pattern. One precision flag: ch.9's sortie-dividing "tired Danish airman" conflates **COMBALTAP** (the actual apportioner, an army/navy 3-star) with **COMAIRBALTAP** — defensible compression, but an airman COMBALTAP isn't the attested pattern.

2. **Air C2 / HURRICANE — SILENT + a CORRECTION.** The book has no offensive-air apportionment conference (confirms our "inference-only" finding). Crucially, **HURRICANE is a two-navy simultaneous HARPOON+EXOCET(+Kormoran-if-feasible) missile ambush, and Figs 18.6/18.7 are naval geometry, NOT air-allocation charts** — corrects the shelf note that treated it as an air/BAI contest. Bonus: the nuclear alternative (SEP BARD BELL E) was **tested Sept 1983**.

3. **Minefields (Fig 18.4) — CONFIRMS + EXTENDS richly.** Nine named Danish controlled fields, German-laid southern fields (Fehmarn, Lübeck Bay), fort/battery cover (Langeland, Stevns), a Counter-Surprise→Simple→Reinforced alert ladder, government owns the decision. Confirms ch.1's "minefields close straits" and ch.15's Belt raid geometry; ferry routes explicitly kept clear of mining.

4. **Ferry / UKMF — RESOLVES the number.** No Storebælt bridge (confirmed); the lift mechanism is the standing NAVBALTAP order "Waterborne Movements." The **29,000/15,000/10,000 tangle resolves as different packages, not contradictions**: 29k = full UKMF (+45 tanks, +52 aircraft), 13,500 = land combat element, 10k/15k = later reduced variants; 9th ID = 27,000. All still TO-VERIFY-PRINT (garbled tables).

5. **NEPS — CONFIRMS + EXTENDS.** 1955, ~140 miles, Jutland airfields, later to German bases, Frederikshavn head, Skrydstrup/40,000 m³ extension. New datapoint: the pipeline is a **struck target** in Exercise DALGAS and in nuclear target lists — which sharpens (doesn't contradict) canon's "red deliberately leaves the in-reach pump stations."

6. **Territorial/Heimatschutz — EXTENDS/CONFIRMS-plausible.** Default: the S-H home-guard brigade is "not under NATO command"; HOLD FAST 1960 recommends the Territorialheer be placed under the joint HQ in war (Kiel-Canal-bottleneck rationale) — the exact warrant for canon's mid-war OPCON order.

7. **LANDJUT HQ — Rendsburg CONFIRMED; Hohn SILENT.** Hohn appears only as a Luftwaffe airfield; no LANDJUT wartime displacement is attested — that dispersal remains our (plausible) invention.

Seven ranked print-check asks for DK are listed at the end of the file, topped by the Fig 9.3 org chart and the Ch.15 UKMF/Table 15.1 numbers.</result>
