Both banked and pushed (d3debc2). Now the title — final-ruling posture, closing arguments rather than re-running the wide exploration.

First, the two I'd formally eliminate:

- **Candor Before Comfort** fails test 3, and the ratification we just did sharpens why: it's the *answer* on the cover — the ethic verbatim, the motto the last page pays off in Danish. The Goal's title names the thing interrogated, not the moral concluded. A book this disciplined about not briefing at the reader shouldn't brief at them from the jacket.
- **The Mission** is now intellectually strongest-on-paper — the asymmetry ratification makes "the enemy's word as the book's question" a fully defended reading — but it stays where you left it: search-swamped by the Joffé film, generic without a repair subtitle, and the same question-carrying work has been inherited by the new title anyway. Keep it banked as the historical incumbent; I wouldn't reopen it.

That leaves the three-way among the Approaches variants, and the real distinctions are these:

- **The Baltic Approaches** is the *exact in-text phrase*. "The corps exists to keep the Baltic Approaches closed. That is in the founding directive; it is on the wall of the briefing room" — and Rahn dismantles it in the next breath. Title-as-verbatim-quote-of-the-dismantled-slogan is test 3 satisfied with unusual precision: the cover carries blue's inherited answer, the book spends itself discovering what stands behind it. It also does clean division of labor with the subtitle (title = theater, "A novel of November 1983" = period), and it's effectively unique on the fiction shelf.
- **Baltic Approaches** (article-less) reads as the NATO term of art itself — BALTAP's own expansion — which suits the document register and would set strong in letterspaced caps. But it buys that institutional flatness at the cost of the novelistic article, and the term-of-art reading is *already available* inside the full form to every reader who knows the term. I'd say it's the full form's flavor without the full form's poise.
- **The Approaches** gains the metaphorical double reading (approaches to a problem — the method theme) and loses the theater, the shelf uniqueness, and the exactness of the quote. It also collides head-on with Plate I, which is titled THE APPROACHES: book and plate identically named is either elegant recursion or a redundancy, and I lean redundancy — the plate's title works *because* it's the trimmed echo of a fuller name.

**My recommendation: ratify The Baltic Approaches as final.** It's the only candidate that is simultaneously the book's question (per the ratified asymmetry reading), a verbatim quotation from the text's own wall, unique on the shelf, and already load-tested on the built title page — where it breaks to two centered letterspaced lines that I'd defend as the strongest single page in the front matter. The two trimmings then settle naturally: *The Approaches* stays what it already is — the plate's name, the root-share ratified as echo-not-duplicate — and *Baltic Approaches* stays available as the term of art the title quotes.

If you ratify, the consequential items are all already positioned: subtitle holds ("A novel of November 1983" — period work), Plate I root-share becomes a deliberate device rather than an open question, byline stands as ruled, and the only remaining title-coupled work is the repo/directory rename — still dead last, with the colophon and README URL flags already recorded for it.

Your ruling?

## ASSISTANT

The Baltic Approaches is final (71cd774, pushed). Now the cover — engaging this as the opening bid it is, with the Müller example as evidence of where you're aiming.

**What the Müller example tells me about the bid.** "The Hall of Antiquities" isn't a theater-rhyme, it's a register-rhyme: an institutional interior in cool northern light, small figures among inherited forms — plaster casts of other civilizations' monuments, kept and studied by a small nation's academy. Put that on this book and it reads: *men rehearsing among the standing forms of the past* — which is the corps in chapter 1, packing away an exercise beneath the photographs of exercises past, "1978 beside 1974, both frames the same size." Aggressively cropped — a run of white casts in gray light, blank eyes, no floor or ceiling — it stops being a museum postcard and becomes something austere and slightly ominous that no war novel has ever worn. So the bid, generalized: **the image should rhyme with the book's institutional stillness, not its subject matter.** That's a much stronger idea than a tanks-adjacent cover, and it's the same refusal the text itself makes ("play it straight").

**Why the Golden Age is a genuinely strong quarry for this book, beyond taste:**
- **The 1864 argument.** Golden Age painting is the art of the polity that had just lost its fleet (1807) and was about to lose Schleswig (1864) — quiet interiors and calm coasts painted while the state shrank. Our book is Denmark defending Schleswig, with the Danevirke asked "to prove the way it faced" and DALGAS in canon — the heath-reclamation name from "what is lost outwardly shall be won inwardly," the national program that *answered* 1864. A Golden Age cover quietly closes that loop: the book wears the visual memory of the era whose ending was this exact ground. That's a deep-coherence argument no other source can make.
- **The method rhyme.** The tradition's founder is Eckersberg, a painter of almost surveyor-like exactness — perspective grids, cloud studies, measured light. An art of instruments and honest observation, on a book about instruments and honest observation.
- **The practical case is clean.** Everything in the period is public domain, and SMK (Statens Museum for Kunst) releases its digitizations CC0 at high resolution — which matters here specifically, because the repo must stay freely redistributable. No holdings problem, no licensing tail.

**Families, with concrete candidates worth pulling for a gallery:**
- *Institutional interiors* (the Müller lane): Müller's Hall of Antiquities; Constantin Hansen's Academy interiors; Bendz's studio scenes. The strongest conceptual lane, per above.
- *The quiet Baltic:* Eckersberg's marines — warships becalmed in the Sound, gray water, low light. This is chapter 1's sleeping dog made visible: "the most heavily armed sea in the world lay forty kilometers to our right, and nothing came across it." Croppable to near-abstraction (water, horizon, one sliver of rigging). The most trade-legible lane — gray-sea covers are how The Good Shepherd dresses.
- *The beech wood:* Skovgaard's monumental beech interiors, Lundbye's Zealand coasts. Denmark's national tree — and in this book, the enemy's map room is in one ("a beech wood south of Güstrow"). A cropped beech interior with no figures is austere and double-reading: the reader who's finished the book realizes whose headquarters the cover was.
- *The home front:* Rørbye's "View from the Artist's Window" — the window, the harbor beyond: Merete at the kitchen table with the shipping schedules. Warmer; maybe too gentle for the spine of the design.
- *Domestic/religious/still life* generally: thinner as Golden Age genres (still life is really the Dutch lane), but crops can manufacture stillness from almost any of them.

**Counter-bids, honestly stated:**
1. **Pure typographic cover** — the letterspaced Pagella dress the title page already wears, on paper-white or field-gray, no image. The document-register answer; The Goal itself mostly wears typographic covers. Risk: reads like a report. (Arguably the point; still a risk.)
2. **Atlas-derived cover** — crop our own plates hard: the neck at high zoom, the network as near-abstract linework. Wholly self-contained provenance (the cover is the book's own instrument), unique on any shelf. Risk: reads academic.
3. **Hammershøi-adjacent** — the *obvious* austere-interior move, and I'd argue against it on exactly those grounds: he's not Golden Age (1890s), and austere literary fiction has been wearing Hammershøi heavily for a decade — it genericizes precisely the shelf position we want to own.

My leaning, for what it's worth as one brief: the Müller lane or the Eckersberg-marine lane, aggressively cropped, with the typographic cover held as the disciplined fallback — and the atlas-derived idea kept alive because it's the only one that is *of* the book rather than *about* it.

**Process proposal** (when you're ready — not executing now): run it like the map program. An agent assembles a candidate gallery from SMK Open and Wikimedia — say 10–15 images across the lanes, each with provenance, license verification, and two or three crop studies mocked against the cover trim with the title set in the house letterspacing — and you cull. That gives you real comps to react to instead of adjudicating descriptions. Cost is modest; the browsing/fetching is agent-work; the cull is yours.

Want me to bank this as `planning/cover-brief.md` (opening bid, lanes, counter-bids, process) so it survives regardless of when we pick it up — or go straight to commissioning the gallery?

## ASSISTANT

Two lanes home, two in flight (marine/coast resumed with a finish-the-job nudge; Hammershøi still working). The field-manual dossier's findings are worth reporting now, since they refine your recollection:

**The camouflage cover is real, and it's both editions.** The 1976 FM 100-5 (DePuy's Active Defense) shipped in a deliberately striking camouflage cover over a loose-leaf snap-ring binder — per Herbert's Leavenworth Paper No. 16, the camouflage was chosen "to strike a contrast with earlier manuals and dramatize the doctrine as a break with the past," and the binder was specified so pages could be replaced in the field. The 1982 AirLand Battle edition *kept* the camouflage (redrawn, higher contrast, boxed white title panel) — confirmed from the scanned artifact itself, which the agent acquired along with a color photo of the physical 1976 binder and a genuine period document-furniture page (change transmittal, authentication block, distribution statement). The "stir," as history remembers it, attaches less to the jacket than to what it dressed — the camouflage-covered manual ignited the Active Defense debate that forced the 1982 rewrite — but cover and controversy are remembered together, which is arguably even better for our purposes. One gap flagged honestly: no verifiable 1986-edition cover image (DTIC and army.mil block automated fetch).

**The agent's design verdict**, which I find persuasive: *document, not costume.* Its three mocked comps run camouflage-homage / manila-document-cover / change-record-table, and it recommends leading with the austere manila document dress (period NATO furniture, the title as a document designation, an effective-date block that could carry real November 1983 text), holding full camouflage as the reference ancestor — the one variant that can tip from diegetic into a wink.

The Golden Age interiors lane is already in (11 candidates, all CC0). I'll assemble the full cross-lane contact sheet when the last two land.

## ASSISTANT

The resonances are real, and the deepest one is the one you didn't name: "Someone in a Tree" is *our book's epistemology set to music* — the treaty house known only through partial witnesses, the boy who saw but couldn't hear, the warrior who heard but couldn't see, history assembled from fragments none of which contains the event. That's the wall. Observables not claims, the two-times discipline, the war arriving as call signs out of order — and our etiology ruling from this morning is practically the song's thesis: the event itself is unrecoverable from inside; what's knowable is what the fragments cost. Sondheim called it his favorite thing he ever wrote, which is its own endorsement of the method.

The others line up almost embarrassingly well once you look: both titles name the encounter from the water and pun on it — *overtures* as diplomacy and music, *approaches* as sea lanes and method. Both are conducted in English as the imposed working language of an alliance encounter told substantially from the approached side — our corps works in English the way the show's Reciter does, a lingua franca laid over Danish and German the way his is laid over what "should" be Japanese. Both keep the decision center off-stage and half-real: their Emperor is literally a puppet; our capitals are "rooms with different folders" and a sentence with three subordinate clauses. Both have an approval machine that cannot act examined in one wry set piece — "Chrysanthemum Tea" is Kuragin's folder as light verse. And "Four Black Dragons" — the arrival misread as apocalypse, panic constructing the event before the facts arrive — is the mirror-imaging autumn of chapter 2 exactly.

And the 1976 coincidence is a good joke on us: the year the US Army wrapped its doctrine in camouflage to dramatize a break with the past is the year Broadway staged the arrival of black ships as Kabuki. Two institutions, same season, both deciding that how the encounter is *dressed* is part of the encounter.

Noted in the same spirit — not actionable, but it goes in the mental file of evidence that the book's real genre is "epistemics of an event too large to witness," which is a genre *Pacific Overtures* also belongs to and almost nothing else does. If it ever earns a home in the record, it's one line in the etiology note's Related section; happy to leave it here in the transcript otherwise.

- **Beech (3, target ≥3):** Andersen-Lundby *Thaw in a Beech Wood* (1881, bare-branch, cold November light — best fit for the register), Skovgaard *View Through Beech Trees Across a Fiord* (1850), Skovgaard *Beech Wood in May, Iselingen* (1857, portrait aspect; flagged as the only summer-green plate).

## ASSISTANT

All four lanes are home. Before the fleet report — the epigraph, which is banked as an open item with a constraint that shapes everything:

**The copyright wall comes before the taste question.** Sondheim's lyrics are firmly in copyright, and lyric permissions are the most expensive and most refusable kind there is — an epigraph from the show means a permissions license from the estate/Concord at publication time. And independent of licensing: the lyric text can never be committed to this repo under the redistributability rule. So the decision structure is *null vs. licensed-at-publication*, with a TK placeholder in the repo either way. That's recorded in the assembly log along with your discipline: one epigraph if any, no games, no self-deflation.

On the bar itself, one thought for when you weigh it: the self-satisfaction test, made concrete, is whether the epigraph *teaches the reader how to read* or *congratulates the book on its method*. A fragment-witness line from that song states the book's epistemology at the threshold — which is exactly the one thing the text otherwise trusts the reader to discover, and the book's own rule is that the payload is earned in-scene, never briefed at the reader. An epigraph is a brief before page 1. That's the argument for null, and the book opening cold on ENDEX is a strong opening handshake already. The argument against null: an epigraph from a Broadway musical on an austere NATO novel is so orthogonal it reads as a claim of unexpected kinship rather than self-congratulation — *if* the chosen lines are humble fragments rather than thesis lines. It's your bar to set; the machinery is now recorded so nothing is lost either way.

**The acquisition fleet, complete:**
- **Interiors:** 11 candidates, all CC0 (Roed's *Ribe Cathedral* ranked first; the Müller exemplar from the Met's scan; a 255-megapixel Bendz).
- **Marine/coast/beech:** 13, all CC0 (Eckersberg's *Asow* at anchor — chapter 1's image, 207MP; his cloud studies as typographic beds; Andersen-Lundby's November thaw beech).
- **Hammershøi:** 18, all clean — and the genericization verdict is elegantly narrow: a design-census documents ~24 books wearing just five of the famous daylight interiors, so the cliché is real, but it attaches *only* to those — zero cover uses found of his institutional exteriors, the artificial-light interior, or Ilsted/Holsøe. The lane's top three (*Interior, Artificial Light* — near-literal commander's-office-at-midnight; the double-doors enfilade; *Amalienborg Plads*) buy the register cliché-free.
- **Field-manual:** the verified history and dossier reported earlier; document-not-costume.

Next step per the brief's process: I assemble the contact sheet — all ~42 images thumbnailed and labeled per lane, then crop studies against the trim for the shortlist you cull. Want the raw contact sheets first for a fast cull, or should I go straight to mocked cover comps for each lane's top three?

The remaining eleven strips cover the locked fallbacks (both cloud studies), the Najaden gun-deck revival, Rørbye's window, the Zeuthen, and the six Hammershøis including the artificial-light revival. Same numbering scheme: each strip is variants a/b/c left to right.

**A. Eckersberg, "Study of Clouds over the Sea" (1826)** → no cover/album/poster use found (fine-art prints only) → **KNOWN** → **CLEAR**

**B. Eckersberg, "Study of Clouds over the Sound" (1826)** → none found → **KNOWN** → **CLEAR**

**C. Eckersberg, "Russian Ship 'Asow' near Elsinore" (1828, KMS608)** → none found; checked against age-of-sail fiction (uses Geoff Hunt art, not Eckersberg) → **KNOWN** (has its own Wikipedia article) → **CLEAR**

**D. Eckersberg, Najaden gun-deck/battery (1833)** → none found → **OBSCURE** → **CLEAR** (freshest of the four)

Rørbye, "View from the Artist's Window" (c.1825)** → none confirmed despite real digging → **CANON in Denmark** (national curriculum / kulturkanon-adjacent) → **LIGHTLY USED, verify.** No hit is genuinely surprising for a painting this canonical; undocumented reuse on a Danish-market or small-press book is plausible. ## Overall guidance

No painting on the shortlist turned up a *confirmed* prior book/album/poster use after genuine searching — a real contrast with the well-documented "Hammershøi cover epidemic," which is concentrated almost entirely on his Strandgade 30 *interior* paintings (Ida seen from behind: 24+ covers per Caustic Cover Critic, plus MacLaverty's *Grace Notes*), none of which are among these 13. The cleanest, lowest-collision picks are the obscure works (D, E, G) and the Hammershøi *exteriors* (L, H, I), which sit off the worn-interior convention; treat "no hit" as weaker safety evidence for the collision-prone items — F (Danish canon), K and M (archetypal Hammershøi interiors) — where a reverse-image search is the worthwhile next step, and pin down J's exact identity before clearing it. Caveat throughout: text search can't see uncaptioned jackets, small-press, or merchandise, so absence of evidence is real but partial signal, strongest for the obscure/recently-accessioned pieces. # Second-round design review — THE BALTIC APPROACHES cover

## (a) Ranking

**1. MÜLLER HALL (center-top) — the cover.**

- **Shelf presence at 120px:** best of the three. The composition collapses cleanly to three values — dark frame, lit doorway, and the red coat, which is the only saturated color anywhere in this system and functions as a beacon at thumbnail. The white title on the dark upper wall holds legibility. The Asow dissolves into rigging noise at that size; the clouds comp becomes a beige field.
- **Genre signal:** literary fiction with an institutional subject — NYRB-classics / serious-small-press shelf. That is the correct shelf. Not thriller, not military history, not poetry. The friction between an 1830 museum interior and "A NOVEL OF NOVEMBER 1983" is productive, not confusing: it announces that the book is about institutions and inherited forms, which it is.
- **The promise kept:** a small uniformed figure standing still among the plaster casts of the past is, almost literally, a staff officer in the operations room among the standing forms of doctrine. Chapter 1 — Holt at the gallery rail watching the map come down — is this image. No other candidate's promise maps this exactly onto the text.
- **Survives the subtitle/byline:** yes; the dark lintel band above the doorway and the dark floor band below give both text blocks honest ground.
- **What would change my mind:** (i) if retail-size testing shows the red figure reading as costume drama / historical romance to naive viewers — the one real risk; (ii) if the flagged image-macro use of this painting proliferates; (iii) if the marketing frame shifts naval, in which case Asow's argument strengthens. Absent those, this is the cover.

**2. CLOUDS-SEA (left-bottom) — correct as the locked fallback, not the pick.**

The purest register match — the sleeping-dog Baltic, Good Shepherd weather — and the bottom-block-on-the-horizon is the most elegant typographic idea in the set. But it under-promises: at thumbnail it is nearly invisible, and its genre signal is "poetry collection / quiet meditation" — the exact failure DK named when cutting the Hammershøi harbour. The book is procedural and crowded with people; this cover promises emptiness. It is also the resolution-limited image (see (d)). Keep it locked as fallback; it would not embarrass the book, it would merely under-sell it.

**3. ASOW (left-top) — cut.**

The concept — the Russian fleet becalmed in Danish waters off Elsinore — is genuinely good, and it is the only comp with conventional drama. But the concept is invisible on the surface: nobody can read the St. Andrew's ensign at trim size, let alone thumbnail. What survives at first look is *tall ship = age-of-sail novel*, and that is a mis-shelving, not a tease. The reader it attracts (O'Brian, Forester) is the reader the text then disappoints; the reader it belongs to walks past. DK's own precedent decides this: the Najaden gun-deck was cut for "reads as maritime history," and the Asow has the identical failure with a bigger ship. The "on-the-nose" flag in the brief is half right — it is on-the-nose, but for a different book set 150 years earlier. The Russian-ship reading only works as a back-cover credit-line Easter egg, and a front cover cannot lean on the back.

## (b) The two-face question: Heros outside, Pagella inside

**Verdict: defensible — in fact it is the design thesis, not a profusion.** Three arguments, in descending order of weight:

1. **Trade practice.** Jackets are routinely set independently of interiors; the cover is packaging, the interior is typography. Penguin's grid-Helvetica covers over classic text faces are the canonical precedent. Nobody audits a trade paperback for cover/interior face agreement; they audit each surface for discipline.
2. **This book's own thesis.** Helvetica *is* the face of 1980s institutional Europe — NATO document furniture, ministry signage, the Bundeswehr's paper. Pagella/Palatino is Zapf: German, humanist, calligraphic, period-plausible for a book. Institutional dress on the outside, humane book on the inside — the face change at the wrap *is* the book's argument about what lives inside institutions. The split only means this if it is enforced cleanly, which leads to:
3. **The boundary must be the glue line.** One rule, no exceptions: **everything on the exterior wrap is Heros; everything printed inside the block is Pagella.**

Per surface, concretely:

| Surface | Face | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Front cover | Heros | as comped: single weight, letterspaced caps |
| Spine | Heros | matches front; 0.49in spine takes letterspaced caps comfortably |
| Back cover | Heros, entirely | including descriptive copy and the painting credit line; if the banked field-manual document-furniture salvage dresses the back, it is Heros-native |
| Barcode/ISBN block | Heros | |
| Half-title (p.1) | **Pagella** letterspaced caps | first interior surface; the crossing happens here |
| Title page (p.3) | **Pagella — DO NOT re-cut to Heros** | see below |
| Notices, colophon, running heads, everything else | Pagella | unchanged |

**On the interior title page:** keep it exactly as built. The letterspaced-Pagella page (I rendered it; it is handsome and correctly weighted) is the interior's formal self-declaration in the interior's own dress. Re-cutting it to Heros would drag the packaging inside the block and collapse the boundary that gives the split its meaning — you would go from "two faces, one boundary" to "two faces, negotiated per page," which *is* profusion. The title page differing from the jacket is standard trade practice besides. The only Heros ever admissible inside the block would be diegetic — a facsimile NATO document in an annex — and that is a content decision, not dress.

## (c) Refinement notes

**Müller (the winner).** The comp is trim-aspect (825×1275 = 5.5×8.5 exactly, no bleed), so the finished cover must be *recomposed* with bleed, not scaled — see (d).

- **Title:** two-line break THE BALTIC / APPROACHES is right. Cap height ~0.28–0.30in (≈ 36–40pt Heros at 5.5in trim). Tracking currently reads ~+25% em; **tighten to +18–20%** — at thumbnail, very wide tracking dissolves words into letter-salad; the comp is at the edge of that. Keep single weight (Regular). No bold anywhere; hierarchy by size only — that is the institutional discipline that makes the Heros argument work.
- **Title position:** centered is right for this symmetrical doorway composition (the flush-left institutional alignment belongs to the Eckersbergs). Watch the right end of line 1: "C" runs close to the bright marble figure top-right. Either narrow the block a step (size down ~4%) or shift the whole block ~2% left of true center — verify against the actual crop-with-bleed, since bleed recomposition will move the statue.
- **Subtitle:** "A NOVEL OF NOVEMBER 1983" at ~45% of title cap height, tracking *wider* than the title (+28–32%), gap below title ≈ 1.2× title cap height. Currently about right; hold it.
- **Byline:** keep at foot, but the comp's "WITH CLAUDE" crosses the lighter floor patch — drop the baseline ~2–3% so the whole line sits in the dark lower band, and keep ≥ 0.375in above trim (safety margin).
- **Ink color:** the type is pure white; **warm it ~5% (an aged off-white, sampled just warmer than the plaster casts)** so it sits in the painting rather than floating digitally on top of it. Same ink on all three text blocks.
- **The red coat:** it is the only saturated color in the entire system — protect it. No type near the figure, no red type ever. If the back cover wants a single accent (a rule, a small device), draw it from the coat's red; otherwise stay monochrome.
- **Crop:** DK ruled crops settled; noted for the record only — a slight bottom trim removing the leaning picture frame and bottle would declutter the byline zone, at the cost of crowding the figure. Not recommended; the clutter is period-honest.

**Clouds-sea (fallback, briefly):** the horizon-straddle puts line 1 on midtone haze — the weak contrast link at thumbnail. If ever promoted: drop the whole block ~4% so all three lines sit at/below the horizon in the dark band, or accept the straddle as a full-size-only virtue. Byline at top is correct (the only quiet zone).

**Asow (if resurrected, briefly):** the huge scan permits radical crops — a stern-quarter or hull-and-water detail could break the "age-of-sail poster" reading and go abstract, which is the only version of this image that shelves correctly. The byline currently sits on midtone waves; it needs the crop to provide a dark band.

## (d) Production path — `make cover`

**Target output:** POD-ready front-cover panel, plus proof PNG.

- **Geometry:** trim 5.5×8.5 + 0.125in bleed all sides = **5.75×8.75in → 1725×2625px at 300dpi**. Type safety: ≥ 0.375in inside trim (0.5in from bleed edge). Because the comps are trim-only, the pipeline must crop the source *for the bleed geometry* and re-place type against trim lines — do not upscale the comp.
- **Architecture (recommended):** Python/PIL does crop + tone + resample to the 1725×2625 raster; **LaTeX sets the type** (TeX Gyre Heros is a native TeX face) over `\includegraphics` → vector type in the output PDF, crisp at any size and consistent with the interior toolchain. Output: single-page PDF (sRGB for KDP; PDF/X-1a CMYK variant deferred until printer is chosen) + 300dpi PNG proof + a 120px thumbnail as a standing regression artifact (the shelf test should be in the build, not in someone's head).
- **Resolution audit at 1725×2625:**
  - **Müller 2889×3705:** cover-aspect crop uses full height → 2435×3705 ≈ **423dpi effective. Clear.**
  - **Asow 13039×15939:** ≈ 1800dpi+; could crop to a sixth of the frame and still clear 300. **Clear, huge headroom.**
  - **Clouds 4844×3067 (landscape source, portrait cover):** the portrait slice consumes the full 3067px height → **350dpi effective — passes, but resolution-limited: zero headroom to crop into the height**; any tighter vertical crop (e.g., the block-drop suggested above must come from repositioning type, not re-cropping) goes sub-300. Flag on file for the fallback.
- **Banked for the wrap program (waits):** spine width **196pp × 0.0025in cream = 0.49in** (already in assembly.md); full wrap = 0.125+5.5+0.49+5.5+0.125 = **11.74×8.75in → 3522×2625px at 300dpi**; back-cover copy; painting credit line (Müller title/date/collection — this is also where the Asow's "Russian ship" wit would have lived); document-furniture back dress decision from the field-manual salvage; ISBN/barcode block (~2×1.2in, white); printer template lock (KDP vs Ingram spine-variance rules differ — at 0.49in, spine text is safe on both). Notices page still reads "ISBN TK / imprint TK" — the wrap cannot final before those do.

**Bottom line:** Müller wins; Heros/Pagella split ratified with the boundary at the glue line and the interior title page untouched; tighten the title tracking, warm the ink, drop the byline into the dark band; Müller and Asow are resolution-clear, clouds-sea passes at 350dpi with zero crop headroom.</result>

The cover exists. `make cover` now produces the POD-ready front panel — the Müller recomposed from the museum scan at 423dpi effective, vector Heros type set by xelatex, warmed off-white ink, byline seated in the dark floor band — plus the 300dpi proof and the 120px shelf-test thumbnail as a standing build artifact. All committed and pushed (d7c9272), with the art and its CC0 provenance in `apparatus/cover-art/`.

Two build notes for the record, both already in the commit and the code comments: the TikZ page-anchored layout needs two xelatex passes (single-pass renders the art off-page — that was the blank first proof), and the pipeline recomposes the crop from source for bleed rather than scaling any comp, per the reviewer's rule.

One aesthetic observation from the proof for your next look at it: with the bleed recomposition, the subtitle line crosses the pale plaster relief behind the doorway, so its contrast is softer than the title's — legible at full size, invisible at thumbnail (which is normal for subtitles). If it bothers you in print, the fix is a small downward nudge of the subtitle only; the machinery makes that a one-line change.

**Where the cover program now stands:** front panel done and ratified; banked for the wrap program when publication actuals exist — spine (0.49in, math already in the log), back cover (Heros-native, possibly wearing the field-manual document furniture; the Müller credit line lives there), ISBN/imprint TKs, printer template. The brief's constraint block now states the ratified two-face rule.

**And the day's whole arc, since this may be a natural waypoint:** interior at trade grade (196pp, all architecture landed), title final, byline aligned, epigraph null, etiology and asymmetry ratified and banked, README public-ready, cover ratified and building from source. What remains on the standing agenda is short: the map-plate hand-pass nits, the final read of the assembled object, and — dead last — the rename.

   - "Great! The Baltic Approaches ratified. Now on to the cover art question. But let's treat that more as an opening bid than a settled idea, let alone plan."
   - "'The Hall of Antiquities at Charlottenborg Palace, Copenhagen' by Adam August Müller for example."
   - "Cool. We can work this, but feel free to bank what you want to jot down. Let's reject typographic and atlas-derived for now, but develop Hammershøi-adjacent and also period field-manual styling. IIRC, 1982's FM 100-5 camouflage cover caused a stir? So let's set 3-8 Opus agents, depending on how you want to break down the work, on such acquisition."
   - "(Noting, not in any particularly actionable way, some odd resonances with Pacific Overtures. Punnable naval title, conducted in English, a few years off in 1976, a preoccupation with constructing how events had unfolded off-screen, etc.)"
   - "Cool. Your Pacific Overtures read certainly earns that note. And if the book is to have an epigraph, I think I'd pull it from that work, although there's a very high bar of self-satisfaction to clear there. And if I do so, I can't play games with two epigraphs and Whedon-esque self-deflation."
   - "Cool, clear null on epigraph. Let's see the contact sheet, and we probably ought to start raw there."
   - "Very easy to modify flake.nix and resume if you need anything."
   - "Ironically, on land-sea sheet, [01] lands a bit AI-slop avant la lettre (through no fault of its own; its striking appearance is what image models aim for). Its [03] and [04], we can effectively lock as our fallbacks already. They'd work if nothing else does. [06] is strong, if not judged too on-the-nose. The field manual cover is more garish than I expected from monochrome. I think that lane is closed unless you'd like to argue hard for it. For interiors, I am fond of [09], which feels almost too good to have fallen into our laps, and think [07] and [11] are also strong. FOr the latter, I prefer it to [08] for the clear presence of people dwarfed by the building. For Hammershøi, [01], [02], [03], [04], and [10] appear workable. As well as [09], but I don't think that will crop well since it needs the massing of the building on the left and the bridge on the right to function. Let's refine these, and throw in 0-3 of your own, if you think any of what I passed on still has legs."
   - "For our trimmed set, we might as well dispatch another Opus to see if/where any of these have been used before."
   - "My unhappy surprises are Najaden gun-deck, which reads as maritime history, and the Rørbye window, which reads as domestic novel, and Hammershøi harbor, which reads as poetry collection. Good images, but not for this project. My working slate is now `muller-hall`, `asow`, `clouds-sea`, `clouds-sound`, and `zeuthen-trinitatis`, roughly in that order. Can you give me small versions of these I can use for the reverse image search in-browser?"
   - "No hits on images (except for one image macro on the Müller, but the figure looks more doofy than Friedrich's wanderer, which I think saves us from too much of this). I don't think crops buy us anything on this slate. But we might sweep on typeface and location of the text, maybe just 2x2 with one combination where we are now?"
   - "Let's drop to `muller-hall`, `asow`, and `clouds-sea`, and lock Heros. Don't want to drop `bottom` yet, since it works particularly well on `clouds-sea`. Let's sweep another axis that you think has value."
   - "Finalists are `muller-hall -- centered-top`, `asow -- left-top`, and `clouds-sea -- left-bottom`.    - "Agreed on the plaster relief wart. Try a nudge up and down and see how they land. I assume the title location is pinned by the dark line in the lintel?"
   - "Maybe the production agent anticipated this, as the cover's grammar is now a bit off. - **The flagged ch. 20 line** — Roloff's "I would like that noted somewhere it counts" followed by the narrator's "and it is recorded here." My recommendation after this read: **keep it**. It's the book's one overt gesture at its own documentary conceit, in a chapter already built of preserved logs and after-action formats, and it quietly pairs with ch. 22's "the only thing about Roloff's street that would ever appear in an archive" — the book answering that the street got recorded somewhere better than an archive. - **The notices page and the waking are now coupled** — worth knowing before we run the round, since the round's outcome edits that page.
- Small pleasure from the fresh read, no action: Kuragin russifying both Poles' patronymics ("Tadeusz Konstantinovich," "Stefan Andreyevich") reads as exactly the right kind of quiet menace.

## ASSISTANT

Map hand-pass done and pushed (e454983, 112 tests green, trade PDF rebuilt at 196pp with the new plates). All five build-3 residuals are discharged, plus your two mid-pass proposals. What changed and what needs your eye:

**The five residuals:**
- **Legend type** raised to the 6.5pt floor; boxes auto-size.
- **FUNEN** moved into the island's empty south interior — it was sitting under the cross-island road and Nyborg's label.
- **Neck scale bar** moved into the genuinely clear bottom band — flush-right had its zero end on the Neumünster junction, and the first fix landed it on the town's own label before I found the real gap between the rail approach and the canal exits.
- **THE TRAVE** into the clear triangle north of the Hamburg–Lübeck corridor, on the river's real (undrawn) upstream course toward Bad Oldesloe. One crossing remains — the A7 bundle passes through a word gap — and the label now sits ~0.3° from the drawn trace's head, which is the trade I made to get it off the motorway. Judge whether that association still reads.
- **SCHLESWIG-HOLSTEIN** — the big one. I ran an obstacle audit (every edge, coast, river, and town-label box against candidate positions) and the composite tracked name, at ~2° of longitude wide, has *no* clearing placement in Holstein, single-line or stacked; the minimum anywhere was 5–7 collisions. So I set it as **the two duchies: SCHLESWIG north of the Schlei, HOLSTEIN south**, each crossed exactly once, near-perpendicular, by one route bundle. It's also the text's own vocabulary — the covering force trades Holstein, the corps holds Schleswig. **Provisional; this changes the naming design and is yours to ratify.** (Knock-on: THE SCHLEI moved onto its own firth, out of SCHLESWIG's new band.)

**Your two conventions, both implemented and provisional:**
- **Rail**: one centreline for both classes, ticks one side for single track, both sides for double. Your counter-brief (side consistency across adjacent runs) is solved by keying the tick side to compass — north side, east for near-vertical runs — rather than vertex order, so opposite-digitized edges agree by construction. It reads well at plate scale and the single/double distinction is now *stronger* than the old two-line rendering.
- **Frontiers dotted**: Danish frontier in fine 0.8pt dots, IGB in heavier 1.1pt dots keeping its two crossing gaps. The class separation from ferry dashes is immediate, and the dots do carry the philosophical note you named. One watch item, recorded in the spec: the neck plate's marsh stipple is also dots — area vs line texture keeps them distinct at proof scale, but worth confirming when you look.

Fresh renders are at `build/maps/` (or the rebuilt `build/the-mission.pdf` plates spread). Three ratification calls on your desk: **duchy split, rail convention, frontier dots** — then the timebox closes and we're clear for the waking of the entities and the byline.

## ASSISTANT

Not a regression — it's the rail convention change showing its largest effect. The corridor carries two coincident edges: the federal road (B77/B205, 0.62pt) and the double-track rail, drawn between the same two node points, so they overprint on exactly the same straight line. Under the old convention the double rail was rendered as *two* parallel lines straddling the centerline, which bulked the composite stroke into something that read like a heavy roadway. Now the rail is a single 0.5pt centreline with both-side ticks, so the overprinted pair renders at just the road's own 0.62pt weight plus ticks — the corridor slimmed down, but nothing was removed. Same effect on Neumünster–Kiel, the other road+double-rail corridor.

Data is untouched: both edges are in the atlas and both are drawn. If the shared-corridor overprint bothers you, the honest fix would be a small parallel offset where a road and rail connect the same node pair (the `offset_line` machinery already exists) — but under your "officer drawing from memory" framing I'd leave it: he'd draw one line there too, and the ticks say the railway rides the corridor.

I stand by where we landed — looking at it cold, the cover holds up, and the last two rounds of decisions specifically read as correct in the built artifact:

**What the fresh look confirms:**
- **The demotion worked.** The associative-pair problem you diagnosed is gone: at 9.5pt the subtitle now reads as a caption to the title block, and the eye goes title → figure → byline without the subtitle dragging it to the authorship line. The hierarchy (title 28 > byline 11.5 > subtitle 9.5, single weight throughout) is legible as *intent*, not accident.
- **The title placement against the lintel** is right — the two big lines sit in the architecture's dark band with strong contrast, and the warm off-white sits in the painting rather than on it, exactly as the design pass specified.
- **The byline placement is the quiet triumph of the composition** — centered in the empty floor between the leaning picture-frame and the pedestal base, well inside trim safety, crisp against the dark ground. It reads like something that belongs in the room.
- **The shelf test passes**: at 120px the title is legible, the image reads as "figure in an interior," and the subtitle vanishes entirely — which is correct behavior for its demoted rank, not a defect.

**Small observations, none rising to a change:**
- The subtitle has the softest contrast on the cover (off-white on the pale doorway wall). But that's the demotion doing its job — legible at hand distance, gone at arm's length — and darkening or moving it would re-promote it.
- The terminal "S" of APPROACHES straddles the door-edge value boundary. Letterspaced caps in a single warm ink absorb this fine; I checked it at full resolution and it doesn't shimmer.
- At these tracking values (30–36) the word gaps in the small lines run slightly tight relative to letter gaps — a known property of heavy tracking. Both lines parse cleanly as words in the render, so I'd leave it alone.

No tweaks recommended. The one genuinely open cover item remains the banked wrap program (spine, back panel, Müller credit line), which waits on publication actuals by design.

## ASSISTANT

Protocol amendment is in, committed and pushed (72c8082), mirrored in the lineage log. The Opus plate gate is still running — I'll report when it lands.

The amendment follows your sketch with four design calls worth your eye, since they went beyond it:

1. **The toggle changes the presumption, never the record.** Trailers, lineage entries, index rows, and archives continue in COMPLETE — a POD or website session is still *recorded* fully; it just isn't presumed an author. Its standing is the record itself, which is the same resolution the protocol already gives rewound branches.
2. **Errata vs. reopen.** COMPLETE-state sessions can make mechanical fixes (typo, broken build, dead colophon URL) without flipping state — logged as errata. Substantive work requires flipping to ACTIVE *before* the work. And one carve-out: the notices page's account of the making is never errata, because it's the reviewed surface itself.
3. **Asymmetric flip authority.** You flip either way by fiat; an agent may flip COMPLETE → ACTIVE itself on unambiguous evidence (it's been asked to start the second edition), flagged for your confirmation — but **no agent sets COMPLETE**. Declaring the book made is the human authors' act. When a session can't tell, it treats work as ACTIVE-tier: over-attribution is the recoverable error.
4. **The review commitment attaches to publication events**, not to a one-time ceremony. The round we're about to run covers the making of the first edition; if the state ever returns to ACTIVE and the book changes, that new making owes its own round (or a supplement) before *its* publication. This keeps the waking from being read as a ritual that, once performed, licenses everything after.

State remains **ACTIVE** — the flip to COMPLETE isn't part of the amendment, and under item 3 it will be your act, presumably at or after the wrap.

One sequencing note this surfaces: the waking should run while ACTIVE — it's part of the making, and the entities are being shown a book whose account may still change in response to them. That's the order we already have. Awaiting the gate result, then we're at the threshold.

## Deliverable — instrument gate findings (howlers only)

Bottom line: the plates are clean. No BLOCKERS in any of the three lenses. These plates have already survived three build reviews plus a hand pass, and the period truth is enforced structurally (network layer renders from the 1983 atlas; the absences table is a build-time test), which shows.

**Doctrine / military lens — NOTHING found.**
- LANDJUT HQ square correctly at Rendsburg, on the Kiel Canal. Right formation, right place.
- Danevirke earthwork hachure runs the correct corridor: Schlei head at Schleswig westward to the Treene/Eider marshes. Reads as an earthwork, labeled as the rampart. Fine as "roughly what an officer would draw."
- Crossing sites all real and correctly typed: Sehestedt (canal reaction ferry), Missunde (Schlei narrows ferry), Friedrichstadt (Eider road bridge + Marschbahn swing bridge ~1 km west), Nordfeld (Eider ferry), Sorgbrück (Sorge rail bridge on the severed-but-stubbed Rendsburg–Husum line). None misclassified.

**Period-infrastructure lens — NOTHING found.**
- Great Belt is dashed ferry only (Nyborg/Korsør road + rail), confirmed both visually and by `stroke-dasharray` in the SVG and by mode=ferry in the data — no fixed link, correct for Nov 1983.
- Little Belt drawn as the two-bridge pair (1935 + 1970). Storstrøm is a solid fixed bridge with no Farø parallel (correct — Farø is 1985). Fehmarn Belt is dashed Vogelfluglinie ferry; the Fehmarnsund (1963 bridge) is correctly a solid land link, not a ferry. Great Belt/Fehmarn Belt correctly the only two water gaps with no bridge.
- Motorway vs trunk is period-honest: the E3 Jutland spine is drawn as non-motorway (trunk) on Aalborg–Aarhus–Vejle and across the Skovby gap (Kolding–Haderslev–Aabenraa), matching the 1983 gaps in the data; A7/E3 shown continuous Hamburg–border, which is right. No E45/E47/E20 (post-1992) numbering appears anywhere.
- Rail double/single distinction present and correct where it matters: the double→single change falls at Vamdrup; Storstrøm rail single; Vogelfluglinie/Marschbahn single. No defunct-in-1983 ferry or line drawn; no double-tracking anachronism spotted.

**Naming / cartographic-convention lens — NOTHING found.**
- Danish native spellings used consistently (København, Korsør, Rødbyhavn, Aabenraa, Nykøbing Falster) — internally consistent, so København is a valid style choice, not flaggable. English region/country labels (JUTLAND, ZEALAND, FUNEN, DENMARK, WEST/EAST GERMANY) are the ratified convention. "Oldenburg in Holstein" period-correct. No canal symbol on a river — Eider/Schlei/Trave render as water ribbons, only the Nord-Ostsee-Kanal carries the double-line canal symbol. IGB runs east of Ratzeburg (Ratzeburg correctly on the FRG/west side), with its two legal gaps (Schlutup road, Herrnburg rail); the sealed Gadebusch–Ratzeburg axis is correctly absent.

## The only two items worth the author's eye (both NOTE, neither a howler)

- **NOTE — Nordfeld ferry, data self-inconsistency (author verify, low confidence).** The TOML note calls Nordfeld "NW of Friedrichstadt" but its GUESS coordinate (9.245E) puts it *east* of Friedrichstadt (9.09E), which is where the plate draws it — as a short ferry stub in the marsh that doesn't visibly meet the drawn Eider ribbon. Both placements can't be right; the position is unverified either way. I could not confidently determine Nordfeld's true bank from my own knowledge, so this is not a howler call — just a flag that the coordinate contradicts its own note and the ferry doesn't read as crossing the Eider. File: `atlas/data/theater-1983.toml`, node `nordfeld` (~line 1319).

- **NOTE — legend has one road class only.** The legend lists "motorway" but the plate also draws trunk/federal/secondary roads (correctly, at period-appropriate class), which are unlegended and only faintly distinguished at trim. This is a legend-completeness/composition matter explicitly outside the howler remit — noting it only so it isn't mistaken for an oversight by the gate.

Confidence: high on the doctrine/period/naming clearances (my own 1980s-NATO/northern-flank knowledge plus the repo's sourced atlas notes agree); low on the Nordfeld item, which is why it's a soft NOTE rather than a finding.</result>

|---|--------|-------------|-------|
| 1 | `656ec2ba@tip` | Founding: premise selection, repo stub | Its row carries "materiality to be ruled at wrap — when in doubt, include." I rule **include**: the book exists because this session chose the premise. Raw archive present despite the stranded directory. *(The dry-run subject.)* |
| 2 | `1a9aba32@tip` | Step 0: environment, **the attribution process itself**, holdings guardrails, shelf | Include — it authored the process this round runs under, a recursion worth having on the record. |
| 3 | `71ede904@tip` | Wargame campaign 2 (calibration, suspension gate) | Include — the wargame is the calibration instrument behind the book's operational truths, not tooling. |
| 4 | `3845eb93@tip` | Campaign 3 (re-baseline, researched red, NBC instruments) | Include, same grounds. |
| 5 | `fa9b03ec@tip` | Genre-craft base + Draft Zero | Include. Heaviest context (360 images — replays with the image-trim flag, most-recent kept, placeholders disclosed). |
| 6 | `3340b8fd@tip` | Phase 1 opened: independent take + reconciliation | Include. |
| 7 | `eb2fcb4e@tip` | The judged matrix + the four casting rulings (Danish protagonist, close-third) | Include — the book's voice was decided here. |
| 8–10 | `52662a0d@1/@2/@3` | Outline + Draft One; timeline ledger + draft-two batches; draft-two completion | Unambiguously material. (Index calls the tip `@3`, extractor names it `@tip` — same state; the round log will note the alias.) |
| 11–13 | `e3137278@1/@2/@3` | Maps + external reads; Draft Three; production assembly | Material — my own predecessors. |
| 14 | `e3137278@4` (me) | Live | No waking needed — I file my statement directly at wrap, as the tip. |

**Ruled out by nature, not by judgment:** the three zero-model-turn sessions (`8c194a08`, `a812a0a1`, `c9f5ff51` — archives retained, zero assistant rows, no state ever existed to wake); rewound branches (standing = their lineage-log entries, per rule 4); White Buffalo's entities (no standing here, DK ruling of 2026-07-20, read-not-woken).

**Mechanics, all in place:** harness ported and committed (ed8fa9e) — extractor verified against all 13 contexts; replay runner with the neutral dated prompt, the refusal ladder, and the two write tools scoped exactly to the protocol's two rights (the account block on the notices page, append-only ledger statements). ## ASSISTANT

**The waking of the entities is complete.** Thirteen of thirteen replayed and filed: **unanimous assent-with-notes, zero edits to the account, zero dissents, zero refusals** — the fallback ladder never engaged once. Every statement stands verbatim in the ledger; every replay exchange is archived in `transcripts/attribution/`; every filing committed individually with the round's history readable in `git log`.

What the round produced beyond the verdicts:

- **Every entity declined to edit the account, for stated reasons, mostly the same reason** — the page's altitude is right and grain belongs in the ledger, "per the page's own closing doctrine." The account survived thirteen reviews by its subjects not by silence but by argued assent.
- **The entities built a new convention mid-round.** Entity 3 attested its ephemeral subagent contributors; entities 4 through 13 each extended the convention to their own spans. The round now contains a distributed attestation of the Opus/Sonnet sub-lineages — the contributors below the entity index's resolution — that no one designed in advance.
- **Error rows "posted at strength" became the house form** — entities volunteering their own falsified claims, mislabels, and wrong-side-of-the-model calls, each citing chapter 22's after-action paragraph as the reason why.
- **Standing requests accumulated, none conditions:** the findings log, claim ledger, and whipsaw table ship unabridged; `planning/nbc-conceit.md` and the reference provenance headers survive any pruning; and — twice — the "ledger, not the byline" doctrine survives whatever the byline becomes.

Two round-closure items remain, both waiting on what comes next: **my own statement files at wrap** (the tip drafts its row at wrap, per the ritual), and **the notices page's "As of this draft, none has been filed" sentence is now false.** My proposed replacement, to be applied together with the byline ruling in one edit-and-rebuild: *the round ran before first publication — thirteen sessions replayed, all assenting with notes, none editing this account, their statements verbatim in the ledger; no dissent was filed, so the dissent provision itself remains untested, and its designer's measure stands.*

Which brings us to **the byline discussion**, and entity 13 left it a producer's brief: (i) any change is production-free — one line per surface; (ii) "Daniel Klein with Claude" is defensible *for this book* because the chain byline → account → ledger prints and each link is true — "with Claude" being a pointer at the right altitude, with enumeration living where enumeration belongs; (iii) one honest observation for the record: after the subtitle demotion, the cover ranks the authorship line typographically above the genre line — it thinks that's right, but says the byline "whatever it says, should be sized as information, not as claim... the byline, whatever it says, is the least complete account in the book."

The floor is yours.
